    MT1 Q1

    Solution ↓

    Let

    \[\boldsymbol{f(x) = 2x^{2} - 5,\quad\quad g(x) = \frac{x + 5}{2x - 9}.}\]

    Find the composite functions and their corresponding domains.

    • \(\boldsymbol{f(g(x))}\)

    • \(\boldsymbol{g(f(x))}\)

    Solution ↓
    MT2 Q1

    Determine which of the following equations are well-defined functions with independent variable \(\boldsymbol{x}\). Explain.

    • \(\boldsymbol{\frac{2y}{x^{2} - 3|x|} = 1}\)

    • \(\boldsymbol{y^{2} + x^{2} = 10}\)

    MT2 Q2

    Consider a cone whose height is double the size of its radius.

    • Express the volume \(\boldsymbol{V}\) of the cone as a function of radius \(\boldsymbol{r}\).

    • Find the domain of the function in a.

    • Use the function in a. to compute the volume of the cone for \(\boldsymbol{r = 3}\).

    Hint: the volume of a cone is

    \[\boldsymbol{V = \frac{\pi r^{2}h}{3}.}\]

    Solution ↓

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    This question type is four separate skills wearing one hat. Read the question and decide which of the four it is before you write anything.

    If the question asks you to… …it is sub-type
    find an exact value of a trig function at an angle outside the standard set \(0^{\circ},30^{\circ},45^{\circ},60^{\circ},90^{\circ}\) B1 - angle decomposition
    find \(f(g(x))\) or \(g(f(x))\) and its domain B2 - composition
    decide whether an equation defines \(y\) as a function of \(x\) B3 - the uniqueness test
    turn a geometric or physical description into a formula B4 - modelling

    B1. Exact trig values by angle decomposition

    The idea. You are only ever given angles that are a whole number of twelfths of \(\pi\). Every such angle is a sum or difference of two angles you already know exactly, because

    \[\frac{\pi}{12} = \frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\pi}{6}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\frac{\pi}{12} = \frac{\pi}{3} - \frac{\pi}{4}.\]

    Procedure.

    1. Strip the minus sign using even/odd. Cosine and secant are even; sine, cosecant, tangent and cotangent are odd.
    2. Reduce to a basic function. If the question asks for \(\csc\), \(\sec\) or \(\cot\), solve for \(\sin\), \(\cos\) or \(\tan\) first and take the reciprocal at the very end.
    3. Decompose the angle into a sum or difference of two standard angles. Verify the arithmetic over a common denominator of 12 before going on.
    4. Apply the sum or difference identity.
    5. Substitute the special values and multiply out each product.
    6. Combine over the common denominator 4.
    7. Rationalise any radical left in a denominator, then sanity-check the sign and size against the quadrant.

    The special angles you must know cold.

    \(\theta\) \(0\) \(\frac{\pi}{6}\) \(\frac{\pi}{4}\) \(\frac{\pi}{3}\) \(\frac{\pi}{2}\)
    \(\sin\,\theta\) \(0\) \(\frac{1}{2}\) \(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\) \(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\) \(1\)
    \(\cos\,\theta\) \(1\) \(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\) \(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\) \(\frac{1}{2}\) \(0\)
    \(\tan\,\theta\) \(0\) \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\) \(1\) \(\sqrt{3}\) undefined

    The identities you must have memorised.

    \[\sin(A \pm B) = \sin\, A\,\cos\, B \pm \cos\, A\,\sin\, B\]

    \[\cos(A \pm B) = \cos\, A\,\cos\, B \mp \sin\, A\,\sin\, B\]

    \[\tan(A \pm B) = \frac{\tan\, A \pm \tan\, B}{1 \mp \tan\, A\,\tan\, B}\]

    \[\sin\, 2A = 2\,\sin\, A\,\cos\, A,\quad\quad\cos\, 2A = \cos^{2}\, A - \sin^{2}\, A = 2\,\cos^{2}\, A - 1 = 1 - 2\,\sin^{2}\, A\]

    \[\sin^{2}\,\frac{A}{2} = \frac{1 - \cos\, A}{2},\quad\quad\cos^{2}\,\frac{A}{2} = \frac{1 + \cos\, A}{2}\]

    \[\sin^{2}\,\theta + \cos^{2}\,\theta = 1,\quad\quad 1 + \tan^{2}\,\theta = \sec^{2}\,\theta,\quad\quad 1 + \cot^{2}\,\theta = \csc^{2}\,\theta\]

    \[\csc\,\theta = \frac{1}{\sin\,\theta},\quad\quad\sec\,\theta = \frac{1}{\cos\,\theta},\quad\quad\cot\,\theta = \frac{1}{\tan\,\theta} = \frac{\cos\,\theta}{\sin\,\theta}\]

    \[\cos( - \theta) = \cos\,\theta,\quad\quad \sin( - \theta) = - \sin\,\theta,\quad\quad \tan( - \theta) = - \tan\,\theta\]

    Rationalising, the one piece of algebra this sub-type always needs.

    \[\frac{c}{\sqrt{a} + \sqrt{b}}\mkern6mu = \mkern6mu \frac{c}{\sqrt{a} + \sqrt{b}} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{a} - \sqrt{b}}{\sqrt{a} - \sqrt{b}}\mkern6mu = \mkern6mu \frac{c\left( \sqrt{a} - \sqrt{b} \right)}{a - b}\]

    B2. Composite functions and their domains

    The definition. \((f \circ g)(x) = f(g(x))\): run \(g\) first, then feed its output into \(f\).

    The domain rule. \(x\) is in the domain of \(f \circ g\) exactly when both conditions hold:

    \[x \in \operatorname{dom}(g)\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad g(x) \in \operatorname{dom}(f).\]

    Feature in the formula Restriction
    \(\frac{\text{something}}{h(x)}\) \(h(x) \neq 0\)
    \(\sqrt{h(x)}\), or any even root \(h(x) \geq 0\)
    \(\ln\, h(x)\) \(h(x) > 0\)

    B3. Does the equation define \(\boldsymbol{y}\) as a function of \(\boldsymbol{x}\)?

    The definition. The equation defines \(y\) as a function of \(x\) when every admissible \(x\) produces exactly one \(y\).

    The pattern to recognise instantly.

    Equation shape Verdict
    \(y\) appears only to the first power function
    \(y\) appears to an odd power, e.g. \(y^{3} = x\) function, because an odd power is one-to-one on \(\mathbb{R}\), so the real root is unique
    \(y\) appears to an even power, e.g. \(y^{2} + x^{2} = 10\) not a function, because solving gives \(\pm\)
    \(y\) under an even root or inside \(|\, \cdot \,|\) usually not a function; test it

    B4. Building a function from a geometric description

    Geometry formulas worth memorising.

    Solid Volume Surface area
    Cone, radius \(r\), height \(h\) \(V = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^{2}h\) \(S = \pi r^{2} + \pi r\sqrt{r^{2} + h^{2}}\)
    Cylinder \(V = \pi r^{2}h\) \(S = 2\pi r^{2} + 2\pi rh\)
    Sphere \(V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^{3}\) \(S = 4\pi r^{2}\)
    Rectangular box \(V = \ellwh\) \(S = 2(\ellw + \ellh + wh)\)

    MT1 Q2. The composites of \(\boldsymbol{f}\) and \(\boldsymbol{g}\), and their domains

    \[f(x) = 2x^{2} - 5,\quad\quad g(x) = \frac{x + 5}{2x - 9}\]

    Answer

    \[f(g(x)) = \frac{- 18x^{2} + 200x - 355}{(2x - 9)^{2}}\]

    \[g(f(x)) = \frac{2x^{2}}{4x^{2} - 19}\]

    4. The composites of \(\boldsymbol{f}\) and \(\boldsymbol{g}\), and their domains

    \[f(x) = 3x^{2} - 2,\quad\quad g(x) = \frac{x + 4}{3x - 1}\]

    Answer

    \[f(g(x)) = \frac{- 15x^{2} + 36x + 46}{(3x - 1)^{2}} = \frac{- 15x^{2} + 36x + 46}{9x^{2} - 6x + 1}\]

    \[g(f(x)) = \frac{3x^{2} + 2}{9x^{2} - 7}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 1 top☰ Contents

    5. The composites of \(\boldsymbol{f}\) and \(\boldsymbol{g}\), and their domains

    \[f(x) = \sqrt{x + 5},\quad\quad g(x) = x^{2} - 1\]

    Answer

    \[f(g(x)) = \sqrt{x^{2} + 4}\]

    \[g(f(x)) = (x + 5) - 1 = x + 4\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 1 top☰ Contents

    6. Which equations define \(\boldsymbol{y}\) as a function of \(\boldsymbol{x}\)?

    Part (a)

    \[\frac{3y}{x^{2} - 4} = 1\]

    Answer

    \[y = \frac{x^{2} - 4}{3}\]

    Part (b)

    \[x^{2} + y^{2} = 25\]

    Answer

    \[y = \pm \sqrt{25 - x^{2}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 1 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    B1. Put the function into standard transformed form

    Every question in this type can be written as

    \[g(x) = a\, \cdot \, F\left( b(x - h) \right) + k\]

    where \(F\) is the basic function. Get it into that shape before describing anything. In this course \(b\) is \(1\) or \(- 1\), so the work is reading off \(a\), \(h\) and \(k\).

    The basic functions and their key point.

    Basic function Shape Key point to track
    \(y = x^{2}\) parabola vertex \((0,0)\)
    \(y = x^{3}\) cubic inflection point \((0,0)\)
    \(y = |x|\) V vertex (corner) \((0,0)\)
    \(y = \sqrt{x}\) half parabola endpoint \((0,0)\), domain \(x \geq 0\)
    \(y = b^{x}\) exponential \((0,1)\), horizontal asymptote \(y = 0\)

    B2. The four transformations, and what each does

    In the formula Transformation Effect on the point \((p,q)\)
    \(x \rightarrow x - h\) horizontal shift \(h\) units right (\(h > 0\)) \(p \rightarrow p + h\)
    \(x \rightarrow - x\) reflection in the \(y\)-axis \(p \rightarrow - p\)
    multiply by \(a\), \(|a| > 1\) vertical stretch by \(|a|\) \(q \rightarrow aq\)
    multiply by \(a\), \(0 < |a| < 1\) vertical compression by \(|a|\) \(q \rightarrow aq\)
    \(a < 0\) reflection in the \(x\)-axis \(q \rightarrow - q\)
    add \(k\) vertical shift \(k\) units up (\(k > 0\)) \(q \rightarrow q + k\)

    Two sign traps.

    • \(x - h\) moves the graph right by \(h\). The sign inside the bracket is the opposite of the direction. \((x + 4)^{2}\) shifts left 4.
    • \(- a\) does two separate things: a stretch by \(|a|\) and a reflection in the \(x\)-axis. Name both.

    B3. The order, which is where the marks are

    Apply the transformations in this order, and say so:

    1. Horizontal shift (the \(x - h\) inside the function).
    2. Horizontal reflection, if \(x\) has been replaced by \(- x\).
    3. Vertical stretch or compression by \(|a|\).
    4. Vertical reflection, if \(a < 0\). Steps 3 and 4 can be stated together as “multiply by \(a\)”.
    5. Vertical shift by \(k\).

    Inside-the-function operations (1 and 2) come before outside-the-function operations (3, 4 and 5), because that is the order in which the formula evaluates.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT1 Q3. Graph \(\boldsymbol{g(x) =3(x+4)^{2}}\) by transformations

    Step 1. Name the basic function. The squared bracket is the tell:

    \[F(x) = x^{2},\]

    a parabola opening upward with vertex \((0,0)\).

    Step 2. Put \(g\) into standard transformed form.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = 3(x + 4)^{2} = a\, F(x - h) + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = 3,\quad h = - 4,\quad k = 0\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q4. Graph \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = -3|x-2|}\) by transformations

    Step 1. Name the basic function.

    \[F(x) = |x|,\]

    a V with its corner at \((0,0)\), going up on both sides with slopes \(- 1\) and \(1\).

    Step 2. Put \(f\) into standard transformed form.

    Answer

    \[f(x) = - 3|x - 2| = a\, F(x - h) + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = - 3,\quad h = 2,\quad k = 0\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) = -2(x-3)^{2}+5}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{2}}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a(x - h)^{2} + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = - 2,\quad h = 3,\quad k = 5\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    2. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) =2|x+3| -4}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y= |x|}\)

    Step 1. Note why the hint matters. A coefficient inside the absolute value can always be pulled outside, because \(|cx| = |c||x|\):

    \[|2x + 6| = |2(x + 3)| = 2|x + 3|\]

    So what looks like a horizontal compression is really a vertical stretch. Always factor first.

    Step 2. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a|x - h| + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = 2,\quad h = - 3,\quad k = - 4\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    3. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) = -\sqrt{x-1}+2}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y=\sqrt{x}}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a\sqrt{x - h} + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = - 1,\quad h = 1,\quad k = 2\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    4. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) =\frac{1}{2}(x+2)^{3}-1}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{3}}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a(x - h)^{3} + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = \frac{1}{2},\quad h = - 2,\quad k = - 1\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    5. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) =3^{x-2}+1}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y=3^{x}}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a\, 3^{\, x - h} + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = 1,\quad h = 2,\quad k = 1\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    6. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) = - |x| +3}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y= |x|}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a|x - h| + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = - 1,\quad h = 0,\quad k = 3\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    7. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) =2(x+1)^{2}-3}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{2}}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a(x - h)^{2} + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = 2,\quad h = - 1,\quad k = - 3\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    8. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) = -3\sqrt{-x}}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y=\sqrt{x}}\)

    Step 1. Identify both reflections. There are two, and they are different transformations:

    Answer

    \[g(x) = - 3\sqrt{- x}:\quad\quad\text{the inner } - x\text{ reflects horizontally},\quad\text{the outer } - 3\text{ reflects vertically}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    9. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) = (x-4)^{2}+2}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{2}}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a(x - h)^{2} + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = 1,\quad h = 4,\quad k = 2\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 2 top☰ Contents

    10. \(\boldsymbol{g(x) = -\frac{1}{3}|x-5|}\) from \(\boldsymbol{y= |x|}\)

    Step 1. Read off the parameters.

    Answer

    \[g(x) = a|x - h| + k\quad\text{with}\quad a = - \frac{1}{3},\quad h = 5,\quad k = 0\]

    3Limits and Continuity

    Part A - The Questions

    MT1 Q4

    Evaluate each of the limits below. If a limit does not exist, explain why.

    • \(\boldsymbol{\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\ \left( 3x^{2} - 2x + 1 \right)}\)

    • \(\boldsymbol{\lim_{x \rightarrow - 2}\ \frac{3x^{2} - 2x - 16}{(x + 2)^{2}}}\)

    • \(\boldsymbol{\lim_{x \rightarrow 3}\ \frac{2x^{2} - x + 1}{x - 3}}\)

    • \(\boldsymbol{\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\ \frac{\sqrt{6 - x} - 2}{\sqrt{3 - x} - 1}}\)

    • \(\boldsymbol{\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\ \frac{\sin(3x)}{x^{2} - x}}\)

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    Substitute first.

    B1. The decision, made from what substitution returns

    Substitution gives What it means Route
    a finite number the function is continuous there done, that number is the limit
    \(\frac{0}{0}\) indeterminate, a common factor is hiding B2 factor, rationalise, or use a known trig limit
    \(\frac{c}{0}\) with \(c \neq 0\) a vertical asymptote B3 check both sides; usually DNE
    \(\frac{\infty}{\infty}\) at \(x \rightarrow \pm \infty\) a degree race B4 compare degrees
    bounded \(\times\) something \(\rightarrow 0\) oscillation, tamed B5 squeeze theorem

    B2. The \(\boldsymbol{\frac{0}{0}}\) toolkit

    \(\frac{0}{0}\) is not an answer. It is a message that numerator and denominator share a factor that cancels. Three tools remove it.

    Factor and cancel, for polynomials. Since both vanish at \(x = a\), both have \((x - a)\) as a factor:

    \[\frac{x^{2} - 1}{x^{3} - 1} = \frac{(x - 1)(x + 1)}{(x - 1)(x^{2} + x + 1)} = \frac{x + 1}{x^{2} + x + 1}\]

    Useful factorings to have ready:

    \[a^{2} - b^{2} = (a - b)(a + b),\quad\quad a^{3} - b^{3} = (a - b)(a^{2} + ab + b^{2}),\quad\quad a^{3} + b^{3} = (a + b)(a^{2} - ab + b^{2})\]

    Rationalise, when a square root appears. Multiply by the conjugate to turn the root into a difference of squares. If roots appear top and bottom, use both conjugates at once.

    Known trig limits, when \(\sin\), \(\tan\) or \(1 - \cos\) appears. These three carry the whole topic:

    \[\lim_{\theta \rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin\,\theta}{\theta} = 1,\quad\quad\lim_{\theta \rightarrow 0}\frac{\tan\,\theta}{\theta} = 1,\quad\quad\lim_{\theta \rightarrow 0}\frac{1 - \cos\,\theta}{\theta^{2}} = \frac{1}{2}\]

    They only apply when the angle inside matches the denominator exactly, so manufacture the match:

    \[\frac{\sin(3x)}{x} = 3 \cdot \frac{\sin(3x)}{3x}\mkern6mu \rightarrow \mkern6mu 3 \cdot 1 = 3\]

    The half-angle identity is what turns \(1 - \cos\) into a square, and it is worth memorising in this form:

    \[1 - \cos(2\theta) = 2\,\sin^{2}\,\theta\]

    B3. The \(\boldsymbol{\frac{c}{0}}\) case, where the answer is usually “does not exist”

    If the numerator approaches a non-zero number and the denominator approaches \(0\), the quotient grows without bound. Whether the limit “exists” depends on the sign on each side:

    1. Determine the sign of the numerator near \(x = a\).
    2. Determine the sign of the denominator just left of \(a\) and just right of \(a\).
    3. Combine. If both sides give \(+ \infty\), or both give \(- \infty\), say so. If the two sides disagree, the limit does not exist.

    The squared denominator is the tell. \((x - a)^{2}\) is positive on both sides, so the two one-sided limits agree. A first-power denominator \((x - a)\) changes sign, so they disagree and the limit does not exist.

    B4. Limits at infinity: compare degrees

    For a ratio of polynomials, only the highest powers matter.

    Degrees Limit as \(x \rightarrow \pm \infty\)
    top \(<\) bottom \(0\)
    top \(=\) bottom ratio of the leading coefficients
    top \(>\) bottom \(\pm \infty\); the sign comes from the leading coefficients and the parity of the degree gap

    The honest method, which is what earns the marks, is to divide numerator and denominator by the highest power in the denominator and use \(\frac{1}{x^{n}} \rightarrow 0\).

    B5. The squeeze theorem, for bounded oscillation

    If \(|h(x)| \leq M\) for all large \(x\) and \(g(x) \rightarrow 0\), then \(g(x)h(x) \rightarrow 0\), because

    \[- M|g(x)|\mkern6mu \leq \mkern6mu g(x)h(x)\mkern6mu \leq \mkern6mu M|g(x)|\]

    and both outer bounds go to \(0\). This is the only way to handle \(\sin\, x\) or \(\cos\, x\) as \(x \rightarrow \infty\): those have no limit on their own, but they are trapped between \(- 1\) and \(1\), and that is enough.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT1 Q4a

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\left( 3x^{2} - 2x + 1 \right)\]

    Step 1. Substitute.

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\left( 3x^{2} - 2x + 1 \right) = 9\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT1 Q4b

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow - 2}\frac{3x^{2} - 2x - 16}{(x + 2)^{2}}\]

    Step 1. Substitute -> factor.

    \[\frac{(x + 2)(3x - 8)}{(x + 2)^{2}} = \frac{3x - 8}{x + 2},\quad\quad x \neq - 2\]

    Step 5. Examine the left side

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow - 2^{-}}\frac{3x - 8}{x + 2} = \frac{- 14}{0^{-}} = + \infty\]

    Step 6. Examine the right side

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow - 2^{+}}\frac{3x - 8}{x + 2} = \frac{- 14}{0^{+}} = - \infty\]

    Answer

    Step 7. Conclude. The two one-sided limits disagree, so

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow - 2}\frac{3x^{2} - 2x - 16}{(x + 2)^{2}}\quad\text{does not exist.}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT1 Q4c

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 3}\frac{2x^{2} - x + 1}{x - 3}\]

    Step 1. Substitute to classify.

    This is \(\frac{16}{0}\), not \(\frac{0}{0}\), so there is nothing to cancel. Go straight to the one-sided analysis.

    Step 2. Left side

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 3^{-}}\frac{2x^{2} - x + 1}{x - 3} = \frac{16}{0^{-}} = - \infty\]

    Step 3. Right side

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 3^{+}}\frac{2x^{2} - x + 1}{x - 3} = \frac{16}{0^{+}} = + \infty\]

    Answer

    Step 4. Conclude. The one-sided limits disagree, so the limit does not exist. This is the first-power denominator behaving exactly as predicted: it changes sign through the point, so the two sides split.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT1 Q4d

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\frac{\sqrt{6 - x} - 2}{\sqrt{3 - x} - 1}\]

    Step 1. Substitute to classify \(\frac{0}{0}\)

    Step 2. Multiply by both conjugates at once

    \[\frac{\sqrt{6 - x} - 2}{\sqrt{3 - x} - 1} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{6 - x} + 2}{\sqrt{6 - x} + 2} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{3 - x} + 1}{\sqrt{3 - x} + 1}\]

    Step 3. Assemble and cancel

    \[\frac{(2 - x)\left( \sqrt{3 - x} + 1 \right)}{\left( \sqrt{6 - x} + 2 \right)(2 - x)} = \frac{\sqrt{3 - x} + 1}{\sqrt{6 - x} + 2},\quad\quad x \neq 2\]

    Step 4. Substitute

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\frac{\sqrt{6 - x} - 2}{\sqrt{3 - x} - 1} = \frac{1}{2}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT1 Q4e

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin(3x)}{x^{2} - x}\]

    Step 1. Substitute to classify.

    Step 2. Split off the standard limit

    \[\frac{\sin(3x)}{x(x - 1)} = \frac{\sin(3x)}{x} \cdot \frac{1}{x - 1} = 3 \cdot \frac{\sin(3x)}{3x} \cdot \frac{1}{x - 1}\]

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin(3x)}{x^{2} - x} = - 3\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q7a

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 1}\frac{x^{2} - 1}{x^{3} - 1}\]

    Step 1. Substitute. Both give \(0\), so factor.

    Step 2. Factor

    \[\frac{(x - 1)(x + 1)}{(x - 1)\left( x^{2} + x + 1 \right)} = \frac{x + 1}{x^{2} + x + 1},\quad\quad x \neq 1\]

    Step 3. Substitute.

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 1}\frac{x^{2} - 1}{x^{3} - 1} = \frac{2}{3}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q7b

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{x^{2}}{1 - \cos(2x)}\]

    Step 1. Substitute. \(\frac{0}{1 - 1} = \frac{0}{0}\).

    Step 2. Apply the half-angle identity. With \(\theta = x\) in \(1 - \cos(2\theta) = 2\,\sin^{2}\,\theta\):

    \[1 - \cos(2x) = 2\,\sin^{2}\, x\]

    Step 3. Rewrite the quotient.

    \[\frac{x^{2}}{2\,\sin^{2}\, x} = \frac{1}{2} \cdot \left( \frac{x}{\sin\, x} \right)^{2}\]

    Step 4. Take the limit.

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{x^{2}}{1 - \cos(2x)} = \frac{1}{2}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q7c

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{6x - 9}{x^{3} - 12x + 3}\]

    Step 1. Substitute.

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{6x - 9}{x^{3} - 12x + 3} = - 3\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q7d

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \infty}\frac{5 - 2x^{3}}{x^{2} + 2}\]

    Step 1. Compare degrees. Numerator degree \(3\), denominator degree \(2\). Top-heavy, so expect \(\pm \infty\).

    Step 2. Divide top and bottom by \(x^{2}\), the highest power in the denominator.

    \[\frac{5 - 2x^{3}}{x^{2} + 2} = \frac{\frac{5}{x^{2}} - 2x}{1 + \frac{2}{x^{2}}}\]

    Step 3. Take the limit of each piece. As \(x \rightarrow \infty\): \(\frac{5}{x^{2}} \rightarrow 0\) and \(\frac{2}{x^{2}} \rightarrow 0\), while \(- 2x \rightarrow - \infty\).

    \[\frac{0 - 2x}{1 + 0} = - 2x\mkern6mu \rightarrow \mkern6mu - \infty\]

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \infty}\frac{5 - 2x^{3}}{x^{2} + 2} = - \infty\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q7e

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow - \pi/3}\frac{\tan(2x)}{3x + \pi}\]

    Step 1. Substitute to classify. The angle is \(2x = - \frac{2\pi}{3}\), and

    \[\tan\,\left( - \frac{2\pi}{3} \right) = \frac{\sin\,\left( - \frac{2\pi}{3} \right)}{\cos\,\left( - \frac{2\pi}{3} \right)} = \frac{- \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}}{- \frac{1}{2}} = \sqrt{3}\]

    \[\text{denominator: }3\left( - \frac{\pi}{3} \right) + \pi = - \pi + \pi = 0\]

    Step 2. Recognise the case. This is \(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{0}\), the \(\frac{c}{0}\) case, not \(\frac{0}{0}\). The tangent is perfectly finite here. So there is nothing to cancel, and the answer will be an infinite one-sided split.

    Step 3. Left side, \(x \rightarrow \left( - \frac{\pi}{3} \right)^{-}\). For \(x < - \frac{\pi}{3}\) we have \(3x < - \pi\), so \(3x + \pi < 0\):

    \[\frac{\sqrt{3}}{0^{-}} = - \infty\]

    Step 4. Right side, \(x \rightarrow \left( - \frac{\pi}{3} \right)^{+}\). Now \(3x + \pi > 0\):

    \[\frac{\sqrt{3}}{0^{+}} = + \infty\]

    Answer

    Step 5. Conclude. The one-sided limits disagree, so the limit does not exist.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q7f

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\frac{\cos(\pi x)}{(x - 2)^{2}}\]

    Step 1. Substitute to classify.

    \[\text{numerator: }\cos(2\pi) = 1,\quad\quad\text{denominator: }(2 - 2)^{2} = 0\]

    \(\frac{1}{0}\), so a one-sided analysis. But note the square in the denominator.

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\frac{\cos(\pi x)}{(x - 2)^{2}} = + \infty\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q1a

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \infty}\left\lbrack \frac{3x^{2} - 4}{4 + 2x + 2x^{4}} \right\rbrack \cdot \sin\, x\]

    Step 1. Recognise that \(\sin\, x\) has no limit. As \(x \rightarrow \infty\), \(\sin\, x\) oscillates between \(- 1\) and \(1\) forever and never settles. So the product rule for limits cannot be used directly; this is the squeeze theorem’s case.

    Step 2. Find the limit of the rational factor. Numerator degree \(2\), denominator degree \(4\): bottom-heavy. Divide by \(x^{4}\):

    \[\frac{3x^{2} - 4}{4 + 2x + 2x^{4}} = \frac{\frac{3}{x^{2}} - \frac{4}{x^{4}}}{\frac{4}{x^{4}} + \frac{2}{x^{3}} + 2}\mkern6mu \rightarrow \mkern6mu \frac{0 - 0}{0 + 0 + 2} = 0\]

    Step 3. Bound the oscillating factor.

    \[- 1 \leq \sin\, x \leq 1\quad\text{for every }x\]

    Step 4. Set up the squeeze. Write \(g(x)\) for the rational factor. Multiplying the bounds by \(|g(x)|\):

    \[- |g(x)|\mkern6mu \leq \mkern6mu g(x)\sin\, x\mkern6mu \leq \mkern6mu |g(x)|\]

    Answer

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \infty}\left\lbrack \frac{3x^{2} - 4}{4 + 2x + 2x^{4}} \right\rbrack \cdot \sin\, x = 0\]

    The justification is the whole point: a bounded factor times a factor tending to zero tends to zero.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q1b

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 5\pi/2}\,\tan\, x\]

    Step 1. Locate the point.

    \[\frac{5\pi}{2} = 2\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}\]

    so this is the point \(\frac{\pi}{2}\) shifted by one full period of \(\tan\), which has period \(\pi\). Tangent is undefined there.

    Step 2. Write tangent as a quotient.

    \[\tan\, x = \frac{\sin\, x}{\cos\, x}\]

    At \(x = \frac{5\pi}{2}\): \(\sin\, x = 1\) and \(\cos\, x = 0\). So this is \(\frac{1}{0}\).

    Step 3. Left side, \(x \rightarrow \left( \frac{5\pi}{2} \right)^{-}\). Just below \(\frac{5\pi}{2}\) the cosine is a small positive number (cosine is positive on the quadrant approaching \(\frac{\pi}{2}\) from below):

    \[\frac{1}{0^{+}} = + \infty\]

    Step 4. Right side, \(x \rightarrow \left( \frac{5\pi}{2} \right)^{+}\). Just above, cosine has turned negative:

    \[\frac{1}{0^{-}} = - \infty\]

    Answer

    Step 5. Conclude. The one-sided limits disagree, so the limit does not exist.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    Problem 1

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 3}\left( 2x^{2} - 5x + 1 \right)\]

    Step 1. Substitute.

    Answer

    \(\lim_{x \rightarrow - 3}\left( 2x^{2} - 5x + 1 \right) = 4\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 2

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow - 3}\frac{x^{2} + 5x + 6}{x + 3}\]

    Step 1. Substitute -> factor

    \[\frac{(x + 2)(x + 3)}{x + 3} = x + 2,\quad\quad x \neq - 3\]

    Answer

    \(- 1\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 3

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 4}\frac{x^{2} - 3x - 4}{(x - 4)^{2}}\]

    Step 1. Substitute -> factor

    \[\frac{(x - 4)(x + 1)}{(x - 4)^{2}} = \frac{x + 1}{x - 4},\quad\quad x \neq 4\]

    Step 4. Substitute again.

    \[\frac{4 + 1}{4 - 4} = \frac{5}{0}\]

    Now analyse both sides.

    Step 5. Left side, \(x \rightarrow 4^{-}\). Here \(x - 4 < 0\) and the numerator is near \(+ 5\):

    \[\frac{5}{0^{-}} = - \infty\]

    Step 6. Right side, \(x \rightarrow 4^{+}\). Here \(x - 4 > 0\):

    \[\frac{5}{0^{+}} = + \infty\]

    Answer

    Step 7. Conclude. The sides disagree, so the limit does not exist.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 4

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 1}\frac{\sqrt{x + 3} - 2}{x - 1}\]

    Step 1. Substitute -> Multiply by the conjugate \(\sqrt{x + 3} + 2\).

    \[\frac{\sqrt{x + 3} - 2}{x - 1} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{x + 3} + 2}{\sqrt{x + 3} + 2}\]

    \[\frac{x - 1}{(x - 1)\left( \sqrt{x + 3} + 2 \right)} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x + 3} + 2},\quad\quad x \neq 1\]

    Step 5. Substitute.

    \[\frac{1}{\sqrt{4} + 2} = \frac{1}{4}\]

    Answer

    \(\frac{1}{4}\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 5

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin(5x)}{x^{2} + 3x}\]

    Step 1. Substitute -> factor -> Manufacture the standard limit.

    \[\frac{\sin(5x)}{x(x + 3)} = \frac{\sin(5x)}{x} \cdot \frac{1}{x + 3} = 5 \cdot \frac{\sin(5x)}{5x} \cdot \frac{1}{x + 3}\]

    Answer

    \(\frac{5}{3}\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 6

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{x^{2}}{1 - \cos(3x)}\]

    Step 1. Substitute -> half angle identity,

    \[1 - \cos(3x) = 2\,\sin^{2}\,\left( \frac{3x}{2} \right)\]

    Step 3. Rewrite the quotient.

    \[\frac{x^{2}}{2\,\sin^{2}\,\left( \frac{3x}{2} \right)}\]

    Step 4. Manufacture the standard limit. The angle inside is \(\frac{3x}{2}\), so write \(x = \frac{2}{3} \cdot \frac{3x}{2}\) and square:

    \[\frac{x^{2}}{2\,\sin^{2}\,\left( \frac{3x}{2} \right)} = \frac{1}{2}\left( \frac{2}{3} \right)^{2}\left( \frac{\frac{3x}{2}}{\sin\,\left( \frac{3x}{2} \right)} \right)^{2}\]

    Step 5. Take the limit. The bracketed factor tends to \(1\), so

    \[\frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{4}{9} \cdot 1 = \frac{2}{9}\]

    Answer

    \(\frac{2}{9}\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 7

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \infty}\frac{4 - 3x^{3}}{2x^{2} + 5}\]

    Step 1. Compare degrees. Top \(3\), bottom \(2\): top-heavy, so \(\pm \infty\).

    Answer

    \(- \infty\); the limit does not exist as a finite number.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 8

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \infty}\frac{6x^{2} - x + 2}{3x^{2} + 4x - 1}\]

    Step 1. Compare degrees. Both are degree \(2\), so the limit is the ratio of leading coefficients.

    Answer

    \(2\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 9

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 0}\frac{\tan(4x)}{x}\]

    Step 1. Substitute -> Manufacture the standard limit. Use \(\lim_{\theta \rightarrow 0}\frac{\tan\,\theta}{\theta} = 1\) with \(\theta = 4x\):

    \[\frac{\tan(4x)}{x} = 4 \cdot \frac{\tan(4x)}{4x}\]

    Answer

    \(4\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 3 top☰ Contents

    Problem 10

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\frac{\cos\,\left( \frac{\pi x}{2} \right)}{(x - 2)^{2}}\]

    Step 1. Substitute to classify.

    \(\frac{- 1}{0}\), the \(\frac{c}{0}\) case, with a squared denominator.

    Step 2. Determine the sign of the denominator on both sides. \((x - 2)^{2} > 0\) for every \(x \neq 2\), on both sides.

    Step 3. Determine the sign of the numerator near \(x = 2\). \(\cos\,\left( \frac{\pi x}{2} \right)\) is continuous and equals \(- 1\) at \(x = 2\), so it is negative throughout a small interval around \(2\).

    Step 4. Combine. Negative over \(0^{+}\) on both sides:

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2^{-}} = - \infty,\quad\quad\lim_{x \rightarrow 2^{+}} = - \infty\]

    Answer

    Step 5. Conclude.

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 2}\frac{\cos\,\left( \frac{\pi x}{2} \right)}{(x - 2)^{2}} = - \infty\]

    MT2 Q6

    · 10 pts

    Find the vertical and horizontal asymptotes of the function

    \[\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{x^{2} - 3x - 4}{x^{2} - 16}.}\]

    Problem 7

    Find all vertical and horizontal asymptotes (and any holes) of \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{2x - 6}{x^{2} - 9}}\).

    Problem 8

    Find all asymptotes of \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{x^{2} + 1}{x - 2}}\).

    Problem 9

    Find all vertical and horizontal asymptotes (and any holes) of \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{4x^{2} - 9}{2x^{2} - x - 3}}\).

    Solution ↓
    Problem 10

    Find all vertical and horizontal asymptotes (and any holes) of \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{x - 1}{x^{2} + x - 2}}\).

    Solution ↓
    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    B1. The procedure, in the order that avoids the trap

    1. Factor the numerator and the denominator completely. Do this first, always.
    2. Find the zeros of the original denominator. Every one of them is either a vertical asymptote or a hole. None can be ignored.
    3. Cancel common factors, and record which factors cancelled.
    4. Classify each denominator zero: if its factor cancelled, it is a hole; if it survives in the reduced fraction, it is a vertical asymptote.
    5. Find the horizontal or slant asymptote from the degrees of the original function.
    6. State everything, including the \(y\)-coordinate of each hole.

    The trap. A factor that cancels does not disappear from the domain. \(\frac{x + 3}{x^{2} - 9}\) reduces to \(\frac{1}{x - 3}\), which looks defined at \(x = - 3\), but the original function is not. That point is a hole.

    B2. Vertical asymptotes and holes

    Let the reduced function be \(\frac{N(x)}{D(x)}\) with no common factors.

    At a zero \(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{=}\mathbf{a}\) of the ORIGINAL denominator Classification
    \((x - a)\) survives in \(D(x)\) vertical asymptote \(x = a\)
    \((x - a)\) cancelled completely hole at \(x = a\)

    The hole’s height is the limit, which is just the reduced function evaluated there:

    \[y_{\text{hole}} = \lim_{x \rightarrow a}f(x) = \frac{N(a)}{D(a)}\]

    B3. End behaviour: horizontal and slant asymptotes

    Compare the degree \(n\) of the numerator with the degree \(m\) of the denominator.

    Degrees End behaviour
    \(n < m\) horizontal asymptote \(y = 0\)
    \(n = m\) horizontal asymptote \(y = \frac{\text{leading coefficient of numerator}}{\text{leading coefficient of denominator}}\)
    \(n = m + 1\) no horizontal asymptote; a slant asymptote, found by long division
    \(n > m + 1\) neither; the graph grows like a higher-degree polynomial

    The slant asymptote comes from polynomial long division. If

    \[f(x) = q(x) + \frac{r(x)}{D(x)}\quad\quad\text{with }\deg r < degD,\]

    then the remainder term tends to \(0\), so \(y = q(x)\) is the asymptote. Verify it by checking \(\lim_{x \rightarrow \infty}\left( f(x) - q(x) \right) = 0\).

    A function can have at most two horizontal asymptotes (one each way), and a horizontal asymptote excludes a slant one. But there is no limit on how many vertical asymptotes it can have.

    B4. Two things worth knowing

    • A denominator with no real zeros has no vertical asymptote at all. \(x^{2} + 4\) is never zero, so \(\frac{3x^{3} - 2x}{x^{2} + 4}\) is defined for every real \(x\).
    • The graph may cross a horizontal asymptote. A horizontal asymptote describes behaviour far out, not a barrier. Only a vertical asymptote is never crossed.
    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT2 Q6. A rational function with a hole and an asymptote

    \[f(x) = \frac{x^{2} - 3x - 4}{x^{2} - 16}\]

    Step 1. Factor

    \[f(x) = \frac{(x - 4)(x + 1)}{(x - 4)(x + 4)} = \frac{x + 1}{x + 4},\quad\quad x \neq 4\]

    The factor \((x - 4)\) cancelled -> hole at \(\left( 4,\ \frac{5}{8} \right)\). The factor \((x + 4)\) survived -> vertical symptote \(x = - 4\).

    Step 7. Find the horizontal asymptote. Numerator and denominator both have degree \(2\), so take the ratio of leading coefficients. Show it by dividing through by \(x^{2}\):

    \[\frac{x^{2} - 3x - 4}{x^{2} - 16} = \frac{1 - \frac{3}{x} - \frac{4}{x^{2}}}{1 - \frac{16}{x^{2}}}\mkern6mu \rightarrow \mkern6mu \frac{1 - 0 - 0}{1 - 0} = 1\]

    \[\text{horizontal asymptote: }y = 1\]

    Step 8. State the full answer.

    Answer

    \[\text{vertical asymptote }x = - 4,\quad\quad\text{horizontal asymptote }y = 1,\quad\quad\text{hole at }\left( 4,\frac{5}{8} \right)\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. Equal degrees, one hole

    \[f(x) = \frac{x^{2} - 4x - 5}{x^{2} - 25}\]

    \[f(x) = \frac{(x - 5)(x + 1)}{(x - 5)(x + 5)} = \frac{x + 1}{x + 5},\quad\quad x \neq 5\]

    Step 4. Classify \(x = - 5\). Its factor survives, so it is a vertical asymptote: \(x = - 5\).

    Step 5. Classify \(x = 5\). Its factor cancelled, so it is a hole.

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 5}f(x) = \frac{5 + 1}{5 + 5} = \frac{6}{10} = \frac{3}{5}\]

    Step 6. Horizontal asymptote. Degrees are equal (\(2\) and \(2\)), leading coefficients \(1\) and \(1\):

    \[y = 1\]

    Answer

    Vertical asymptote \(x = - 5\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 1\); hole at \(\left( 5,\frac{3}{5} \right)\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    2. Two vertical asymptotes, nothing cancels

    \[f(x) = \frac{2x^{2} + 3x - 2}{x^{2} - 1}\]

    Step 1. Factor the numerator. Look for factors of \(2 \cdot ( - 2) = - 4\) that add to \(3\): these are \(4\) and \(- 1\). Split and group:

    \[f(x) = \frac{(2x - 1)(x + 2)}{(x - 1)(x + 1)}\]

    Step 2. Look for common factors. Nothing cancels, so there are no holes.

    Step 5. Vertical asymptotes.

    \[x = 1\quad\text{and}\quad x = - 1\]

    Step 6. Horizontal asymptote. Degrees equal, leading coefficients \(2\) and \(1\):

    \[y = \frac{2}{1} = 2\]

    Answer

    Vertical asymptotes \(x = 1\) and \(x = - 1\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 2\); no holes.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    3. Bottom-heavy, with a hole

    \[f(x) = \frac{x + 3}{x^{2} - 9}\]

    Step 1. Factor

    \[f(x) = \frac{x + 3}{(x - 3)(x + 3)} = \frac{1}{x - 3},\quad\quad x \neq - 3\]

    Step 4. Classify \(x = 3\). Its factor survives, so it is a vertical asymptote: \(x = 3\).

    Step 5. Classify \(x = - 3\). Its factor cancelled, so it is a hole:

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow - 3}f(x) = \frac{1}{- 3 - 3} = - \frac{1}{6}\]

    Step 6. Horizontal asymptote. Numerator degree \(1\), denominator degree \(2\): bottom-heavy.

    \[y = 0\]

    Answer

    Vertical asymptote \(x = 3\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 0\); hole at \(\left( - 3, - \frac{1}{6} \right)\).

    This is the clearest example of the trap: the reduced formula \(\frac{1}{x - 3}\) is perfectly happy at \(x = - 3\), but the original function is not defined there.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    4. No vertical asymptote, and a slant

    \[f(x) = \frac{3x^{3} - 2x}{x^{2} + 4}\]

    Step 1. Look for vertical asymptotes. Set the denominator to zero:

    \[x^{2} + 4 = 0\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x^{2} = - 4\]

    So there are no vertical asymptotes and no holes.

    Step 2. Compare degrees. Numerator degree \(3\), denominator degree \(2\). Since \(3 = 2 + 1\), expect a slant asymptote and no horizontal one.

    Step 3. Divide by long division. Ask what times \(x^{2}\) gives \(3x^{3}\): the answer is \(3x\). Multiply back and subtract:

    \[3x\left( x^{2} + 4 \right) = 3x^{3} + 12x\]

    \[\left( 3x^{3} - 2x \right) - \left( 3x^{3} + 12x \right) = - 14x\]

    The remainder \(- 14x\) has degree \(1\), less than the divisor’s degree \(2\), so the division stops.

    Step 4. Write the result.

    \[f(x) = 3x + \frac{- 14x}{x^{2} + 4}\]

    Step 5. Check that the remainder term dies. Numerator degree \(1\), denominator degree \(2\), so it is bottom-heavy:

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \pm \infty}\frac{- 14x}{x^{2} + 4} = 0\]

    Step 6. State the asymptote.

    \[\text{slant asymptote: }y = 3x\]

    Answer

    No vertical asymptotes, no horizontal asymptote, slant asymptote \(y = 3x\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    5. Two vertical asymptotes, equal degrees

    \[f(x) = \frac{5x^{2}}{x^{2} + x - 6}\]

    Step 1. Factor the denominator.

    \[x^{2} + x - 6 = (x + 3)(x - 2)\]

    Step 2. Zeros of the denominator. \(x = - 3\) and \(x = 2\).

    Step 3. Look for common factors: nothing cancels and there are no holes.

    Step 4. Vertical asymptotes.

    \[x = - 3\quad\text{and}\quad x = 2\]

    Step 5. Horizontal asymptote. Degrees equal, leading coefficients \(5\) and \(1\):

    \[y = 5\]

    Answer

    Vertical asymptotes \(x = - 3\) and \(x = 2\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 5\); no holes.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    6. Equal degrees, one hole

    \[f(x) = \frac{x^{2} + 2x - 3}{x^{2} - 1}\]

    Step 1. Factor both

    \[f(x) = \frac{(x + 3)(x - 1)}{(x - 1)(x + 1)} = \frac{x + 3}{x + 1},\quad\quad x \neq 1\]

    Step 4. Classify \(x = - 1\). Its factor survives -> Vertical asymptote \(x = - 1\).

    Step 5. Classify \(x = 1\). Its factor cancelled, so it is a hole (1, 2)

    Step 6. Horizontal asymptote. Degrees equal, leading coefficients \(1\) and \(1\): \(y = 1\).

    Answer

    Vertical asymptote \(x = - 1\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 1\); hole at \((1,2)\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    7. Bottom-heavy, with a hole

    \[f(x) = \frac{2x - 6}{x^{2} - 9}\]

    Step 1. Factor both.

    \[f(x) = \frac{2(x - 3)}{(x - 3)(x + 3)} = \frac{2}{x + 3},\quad\quad x \neq 3\]

    Step 4. Classify \(x = - 3\). Factor survives -> Vertical asymptote \(x = - 3\).

    Step 5. Classify \(x = 3\). Factor cancelled, so it is a hole at \(\left( 3,\frac{1}{3} \right)\)

    Step 6. Horizontal asymptote. Numerator degree \(1\), denominator degree \(2\): \(y = 0\).

    Answer

    Vertical asymptote \(x = - 3\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 0\); hole at \(\left( 3,\frac{1}{3} \right)\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    8. A slant asymptote by long division

    \[f(x) = \frac{x^{2} + 1}{x - 2}\]

    Step 1. Vertical asymptote.

    \[\text{vertical asymptote: }x = 2\]

    Step 2. Compare degrees. Numerator degree \(2\), denominator degree \(1\). Since \(2 = 1 + 1\), expect a slant asymptote.

    Polynomial long division

    1. Check degrees: If the numerator’s degree is greater than or equal to the denominator’s degree, divide.

    2. Divide the leading terms:
      Divide the first term of the numerator by the first term of the denominator.

    3. Multiply and subtract:
      Multiply the result by the whole denominator, then subtract from the numerator.

    4. Repeat until the remainder has degree less than the denominator.

    5. Write the result as

    \[\text{quotient} + \frac{\text{remainder}}{\text{divisor}}. \]

    Step 3. Divide by long division. What times \(x\) gives \(x^{2}\)? Answer \(x\). Multiply back and subtract:

    \[x(x - 2) = x^{2} - 2x,\quad\quad\left( x^{2} + 1 \right) - \left( x^{2} - 2x \right) = 2x + 1\]

    Now what times \(x\) gives \(2x\)? Answer \(2\):

    \[2(x - 2) = 2x - 4,\quad\quad(2x + 1) - (2x - 4) = 5\]

    The remainder \(5\) has degree \(0\), less than the divisor’s degree \(1\), so the division stops.

    Step 4. Write the result.

    \[f(x) = x + 2 + \frac{5}{x - 2}\]

    Step 5. Check the remainder dies.

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \pm \infty}\frac{5}{x - 2} = 0\]

    Step 6. State the asymptote.

    \[\text{slant asymptote: }y = x + 2\]

    Answer

    Vertical asymptote \(x = 2\); no horizontal asymptote; slant asymptote \(y = x + 2\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    9. A hole at a fractional value of x

    \[f(x) = \frac{4x^{2} - 9}{2x^{2} - x - 3}\]

    Step 1. Factor

    \[f(x) = \frac{(2x - 3)(2x + 3)}{(2x - 3)(x + 1)} = \frac{2x + 3}{x + 1},\quad\quad x \neq \frac{3}{2}\]

    Step 5. Classify \(x = - 1\). Factor survives. Vertical asymptote \(x = - 1\).

    Step 6. Classify \(x = \frac{3}{2}\). Factor cancelled, so it is a hole:

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 3/2}f(x) = \frac{2\left( \frac{3}{2} \right) + 3}{\frac{3}{2} + 1} = \frac{3 + 3}{\frac{5}{2}} = \frac{6}{\frac{5}{2}} = \frac{12}{5}\]

    Step 7. Horizontal asymptote. Degrees equal, leading coefficients \(4\) and \(2\):

    \[y = \frac{4}{2} = 2\]

    Answer

    Vertical asymptote \(x = - 1\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 2\); hole at \(\left( \frac{3}{2},\frac{12}{5} \right)\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 4 top☰ Contents

    10. Bottom-heavy, with a hole

    \[f(x) = \frac{x - 1}{x^{2} + x - 2}\]

    Step 1. Factor

    \[f(x) = \frac{x - 1}{(x + 2)(x - 1)} = \frac{1}{x + 2},\quad\quad x \neq 1\]

    Step 4. Classify \(x = - 2\). Factor survives. Vertical asymptote \(x = - 2\).

    Step 5. Classify \(x = 1\). Factor cancelled, so it is a hole:

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow 1}f(x) = \frac{1}{1 + 2} = \frac{1}{3}\]

    Step 6. Horizontal asymptote. Numerator degree \(1\), denominator degree \(2\): \(y = 0\).

    Answer

    Vertical asymptote \(x = - 2\); horizontal asymptote \(y = 0\); hole at \(\left( 1,\frac{1}{3} \right)\).

    The population \(\boldsymbol{P}\) of a city in year \(\boldsymbol{y}\) is \(\boldsymbol{P(y)}\). Write a sentence (in layman’s terms) explaining the meaning of:

    • \(\boldsymbol{\frac{P(15) - P(2)}{13} = 1,431}\)

    • \(\boldsymbol{P'(10) = 14,000}\)

    Solution ↓

    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 6 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    No calculation is required here at all. The marks are for a sentence that a non-mathematician would understand, and that gets four specific things right.

    B1. The two objects, and the one difference between them

    \[\underset{\text{average rate of change}}{\underbrace{\frac{f(b) - f(a)}{b - a}}}\quad\quad\quad\quad\underset{\text{instantaneous rate of change}}{\underbrace{f'(c)}}\]

    Average rate of change Instantaneous rate of change
    Formula \(\frac{f(b) - f(a)}{b - a}\) \(f'(c) = \lim_{h \rightarrow 0}\frac{f(c + h) - f(c)}{h}\)
    Time span a whole interval from \(a\) to \(b\) a single instant \(c\)
    On the graph slope of the secant line through \((a,f(a))\) and \((b,f(b))\) slope of the tangent line at \((c,f(c))\)
    In words “on average, per unit of time, over that period” “at that moment, the rate is”

    The average rate does not say what happened inside the interval; the population could have fallen for five years and then surged. The instantaneous rate is a snapshot, and says nothing about any other moment.

    B2. The four things your sentence must contain

    1. The quantity, named in plain words, not as “\(P\)”. Say “the city’s population”.
    2. The number, with its units. The units are always \(\frac{\text{units of the output}}{\text{units of the input}}\), so a population in people over a time in years gives people per year.
    3. The time reference. For an average rate, name the interval. For a derivative, name the instant.
    4. The direction, if the number is negative. A negative rate means the quantity is decreasing, and the sentence should say so in words rather than leaving the minus sign to do the work.

    B3. Two sentence templates

    Average rate of change over the interval from \(a\) to \(b\):

    Between time \(a\) and time \(b\), [the quantity] changed by an average of [number] [units] per [time unit].

    Instantaneous rate of change at \(c\):

    At the instant \(t = c\), [the quantity] was [increasing / decreasing] at a rate of [number] [units] per [time unit].

    B4. The mistakes that cost the marks

    • Leaving out the units. “\(1,431\)” alone is not an interpretation.
    • Saying “the slope is…”. That describes the graph, not the situation. The question says layman’s terms.
    • Confusing the two. An average rate over \(\lbrack 2,15\rbrack\) is not “the rate in year 15”.
    • Reading a negative rate as a negative quantity. \(T'(3) = - 6\) does not mean the coffee is at \(- 6{^\circ}\)C. It means it is cooling by \(6{^\circ}\)C per minute at that moment.
    • Forgetting that the derivative’s units differ from the function’s. \(V\) is in litres; \(V'\) is in litres per minute.
    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 6 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT2 Q3. City population \(\boldsymbol{P(y)}\) in year \(\boldsymbol{y}\)

    Part (a)

    \[\frac{P(15) - P(2)}{13} = 1,431\]

    Answer

    Between year \(2\) and year \(15\), the city’s population grew by an average of \(1,431\) people per year.

    Part (b)

    \[P'(10) = 14,000\]

    Answer

    In year \(10\), the city’s population was growing at a rate of \(14,000\) people per year.

    (b) \(P'(5) = - 1.5\)

    Answer

    Five days after the announcement, the stock price was falling at a rate of $1.50 per day.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 6 top☰ Contents

    Answer

    Between year \(10\) and year \(20\), the park’s wolf population grew by an average of \(6\) wolves per year.

    In year \(15\), the park’s wolf population was growing at a rate of \(9\) wolves per year.

    7The Derivative from Its Definition

    Part A - The Questions

    F1 Q3

    Use the definition of the derivative as a limit to compute the derivative of \(\boldsymbol{\cot\, x}\).

    Solution ↓

    10 New Practice Problems

    Solution ↓
    Problem 9

    Problem 10

    \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = 3x^{2} + 2x - 1}\)

    Solution ↓
    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 7 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    B1. The definition

    \[f'(x) = \lim_{h \rightarrow 0}\frac{f(x + h) - f(x)}{h}\]

    B3. Which tool clears the \(\boldsymbol{h}\), by the shape of \(\boldsymbol{f}\)

    Shape of \(f\) Tool for step 3
    polynomial expand and collect; every surviving term has an \(h\)
    \(\frac{1}{\text{something}}\) combine the two fractions over a common denominator
    \(\sqrt{\text{something}}\) multiply by the conjugate \(\frac{\sqrt{\ } + \sqrt{\ }}{\sqrt{\ } + \sqrt{\ }}\)
    trigonometric apply an addition identity, then split into the two standard limits

    The binomial expansions worth knowing cold:

    \[(x + h)^{2} = x^{2} + 2xh + h^{2},\quad\quad(x + h)^{3} = x^{3} + 3x^{2}h + 3xh^{2} + h^{3}\]

    The two standard limits every trig case needs:

    \[\lim_{h \rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin\, h}{h} = 1,\quad\quad\lim_{h \rightarrow 0}\frac{\cos\, h - 1}{h} = 0\]

    The addition identities:

    \[\sin(x + h) = \sin\, x\,\cos\, h + \cos\, x\,\sin\, h,\quad\quad \cos(x + h) = \cos\, x\,\cos\, h - \sin\, x\,\sin\, h\]

    B4. The trig pattern, which is the same every time

    For \(\sin\), \(\cos\), \(\tan\) and \(\cot\) the work always goes:

    1. Expand \(f(x + h)\) with an addition identity.
    2. Group the terms so that \(\cos\, h - 1\) and \(\sin\, h\) each appear as a separate factor.
    3. Split the quotient into two pieces, one multiplied by \(\frac{\cos\, h - 1}{h}\) and one by \(\frac{\sin\, h}{h}\).
    4. Take the limits: the first piece dies (it is multiplied by \(0\)), the second survives (multiplied by \(1\)).

    For \(\tan\) and \(\cot\) it is usually easier to combine over a common denominator first, using \(\sin(A - B)\) to collapse the numerator, than to expand the addition formula for the tangent itself.

    B5. The identity that does the work for \(\boldsymbol{\tan}\) and \(\boldsymbol{\cot}\)

    \[\sin(A - B) = \sin\, A\,\cos\, B - \cos\, A\,\sin\, B\]

    Read right to left, it turns the messy numerator \(\sin\, x\, \cos(x + h) - \cos\, x\, \sin(x + h)\) into the single term \(\sin\,\left( x - (x + h) \right) = \sin( - h) = - \sin\, h\). That collapse is the whole trick.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 7 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    F1 Q3. Derivative of \(\boldsymbol{\cot\, x}\) from the definition

    Step 1. Write the definition for this function.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\,\cot\, x = \lim_{h \rightarrow 0}\frac{\cot(x + h) - \cot\, x}{h}\]

    Step 2. Write each cotangent as a quotient. Working with \(\sin\) and \(\cos\) is far easier than using an addition formula for \(\cot\) itself:

    \[\cot(x + h) - \cot\, x = \frac{\cos(x + h)}{\sin(x + h)} - \frac{\cos\, x}{\sin\, x}\]

    Step 3. Combine over the common denominator \(\sin(x + h)\sin\, x\).

    \[= \frac{\cos(x + h)\sin\, x - \cos\, x\, \sin(x + h)}{\sin(x + h)\sin\, x}\]

    Step 4. Collapse the numerator with the sine subtraction identity. The numerator has exactly the shape \(\sin\, A\,\cos\, B - \cos\, A\,\sin\, B\) with \(A = x\) and \(B = x + h\):

    \[\cos(x + h)\sin\, x - \cos\, x\, \sin(x + h) = \sin\,\left( x - (x + h) \right) = \sin( - h)\]

    Step 5. Use that sine is odd.

    \[\sin( - h) = - \sin\, h\]

    so the whole numerator is just \(- \sin\, h\), and the difference is

    \[\cot(x + h) - \cot\, x = \frac{- \sin\, h}{\sin(x + h)\sin\, x}\]

    Step 6. Divide by \(h\), which is the definition’s remaining instruction.

    \[\frac{\cot(x + h) - \cot\, x}{h} = \frac{- \sin\, h}{h\, \sin(x + h)\sin\, x} = - \frac{\sin\, h}{h} \cdot \frac{1}{\sin(x + h)\sin\, x}\]

    The \(h\) is now attached to \(\sin\, h\) alone, which is exactly what the standard limit needs.

    Step 7. Take the limit factor by factor.

    \[\lim_{h \rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin\, h}{h} = 1,\quad\quad\lim_{h \rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\sin(x + h)\sin\, x} = \frac{1}{\sin\, x \cdot \sin\, x} = \frac{1}{\sin^{2}\, x}\]

    the second because \(\sin\) is continuous, so \(\sin(x + h) \rightarrow \sin\, x\).

    Step 8. Multiply.

    \[- 1 \cdot \frac{1}{\sin^{2}\, x} = - \frac{1}{\sin^{2}\, x}\]

    Step 9. Convert to the standard form. Since \(\csc\, x = \frac{1}{\sin\, x}\):

    Answer

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\,\cot\, x = - \csc^{2}\, x\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 7 top☰ Contents

    Given \(\boldsymbol{f(2) = 4}\), \(\boldsymbol{g(2) = - 1}\), \(\boldsymbol{f'(x) = x^{2}}\), \(\boldsymbol{g'(x) = \cos\,\left( \frac{\pi x}{2} \right)}\), find \(\boldsymbol{\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \right\rbrack}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x = 2}\).

    Solution ↓
    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    B1. Read the structure before differentiating

    Look at the outermost operation of the formula. That decides the first rule to apply; everything inside is handled afterwards.

    Outermost structure First rule
    a sum of terms differentiate term by term
    a product of two factors product rule
    one thing over another quotient rule
    a function of a function chain rule
    a constant times something pull the constant out

    B2. The rules

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\lbrack u \pm v\rbrack = u' \pm v'\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\lbrack cu\rbrack = cu'\]

    \[\text{Product: }\frac{d}{dx}\lbrack uv\rbrack = u'v + uv'\]

    \[\text{Quotient: }\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \frac{u}{v} \right\rbrack = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^{2}}\]

    \[\text{Chain: }\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack f(g(x)) \right\rbrack = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)\]

    The quotient rule’s order matters: “low d-high minus high d-low, over low squared”.

    B3. The derivative table

    \[\frac{d}{dx}x^{n} = nx^{n - 1}\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\sqrt{x} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{x} = - \frac{1}{x^{2}}\]

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\,\sin\, x = \cos\, x\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\,\cos\, x = - \sin\, x\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\,\tan\, x = \sec^{2}\, x\]

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\,\cot\, x = - \csc^{2}\, x\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\,\sec\, x = \sec\, x\,\tan\, x\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\,\csc\, x = - \csc\, x\,\cot\, x\]

    \[\frac{d}{dx}e^{x} = e^{x}\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\,\ln\, x = \frac{1}{x}\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}a^{x} = a^{x}\,\ln\, a\]

    The co-functions all carry a minus sign: \(\cos\), \(\cot\) and \(\csc\). That one pattern covers three rows of the table.

    B4. Rewriting before differentiating, which often saves the quotient rule

    \[\frac{1}{x^{n}} = x^{- n},\quad\quad\sqrt[n]{x^{m}} = x^{m/n},\quad\quad\frac{u}{c} = \frac{1}{c}\, u\ \ (c\text{ constant})\]

    \[\frac{a + b}{c} = \frac{a}{c} + \frac{b}{c}\quad\text{(splitting a sum over a single denominator)}\]

    B5. Higher derivatives

    \(\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\) means differentiate, simplify, then differentiate again.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT1 Q5a. \(\boldsymbol{y=4x^{5}+3x^{4}-6x^{3}+6}\)

    Answer

    \[y' = 20x^{4} + 12x^{3} - 18x^{2}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    MT1 Q5b

    \[y = \frac{2x - 16}{(x + 3)^{2}}\]

    Step 1. Identify the structure. One expression over another: quotient rule, with

    \[u = 2x - 16,\quad\quad v = (x + 3)^{2}\]

    Step 2. Differentiate the pieces.

    \[u' = 2,\quad\quad v' = 2(x + 3)\quad\text{(chain rule on the square)}\]

    Step 3. Apply the quotient rule, in the right order.

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^{2}} = \frac{2(x + 3)^{2} - (2x - 16) \cdot 2(x + 3)}{(x + 3)^{4}}\]

    \[y' = \frac{- 2x + 38}{(x + 3)^{3}} = \frac{2(19 - x)}{(x + 3)^{3}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    MT1 Q5c. \(\boldsymbol{y= \sin\,\left( 2x^{2}-x+1 \right)}\)

    Step 1. Identify the structure. A function of a function: chain rule, with

    \[\text{outer }f(u) = \sin\, u,\quad\quad\text{inner }u = 2x^{2} - x + 1\]

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer function, keeping the inner one intact.

    \[f'(u) = \cos\, u\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \cos\,\left( 2x^{2} - x + 1 \right)\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner function.

    \[u' = 4x - 1\]

    Step 4. Multiply.

    Answer

    \[y' = (4x - 1)\cos\,\left( 2x^{2} - x + 1 \right)\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    MT1 Q5d. \(\boldsymbol{y=\left( -4x^{3}-x^{2}+3x+7 \right)^{4}}\)

    Step 1. Identify the structure. A bracket raised to a power: chain rule with the power rule outside.

    \[\text{outer }f(u) = u^{4},\quad\quad\text{inner }u = - 4x^{3} - x^{2} + 3x + 7\]

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer function.

    \[f'(u) = 4u^{3}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 4\left( - 4x^{3} - x^{2} + 3x + 7 \right)^{3}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner function.

    \[u' = - 12x^{2} - 2x + 3\]

    Step 4. Multiply.

    Answer

    \[y' = 4\left( - 4x^{3} - x^{2} + 3x + 7 \right)^{3}\left( - 12x^{2} - 2x + 3 \right)\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q9a

    \[\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\, \cot(2x)\]

    Step 1. First derivative, by the chain rule. Outer \(\cot\, u\) with \(u = 2x\), so \(u' = 2\):

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\, \cot(2x) = - \csc^{2}(2x) \cdot 2 = - 2\,\csc^{2}(2x)\]

    Step 2. Set up the second derivative.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack - 2\,\csc^{2}(2x) \right\rbrack\]

    This is a chain rule inside a chain rule: a square, of a cosecant, of \(2x\).

    Step 3. Differentiate the outermost square. Write it as \(- 2\lbrack \csc(2x)\rbrack^{2}\) and apply the power rule:

    \[- 2 \cdot 2\, \csc(2x) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \csc(2x) \right\rbrack = - 4\, \csc(2x) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \csc(2x) \right\rbrack\]

    Step 4. Differentiate \(\csc(2x)\), chain rule again.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\, \csc(2x) = - \csc(2x)\cot(2x) \cdot 2 = - 2\, \csc(2x)\cot(2x)\]

    Step 5. Substitute back and simplify the signs.

    \[- 4\, \csc(2x) \cdot \left\lbrack - 2\, \csc(2x)\cot(2x) \right\rbrack = 8\,\csc^{2}(2x)\cot(2x)\]

    Answer

    \[\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\, \cot(2x) = 8\,\csc^{2}(2x)\cot(2x)\]

    The two minus signs, one from \(\csc'\) and one already present, cancel to leave a positive result.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q9b

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\,\sec\,\left( x^{2} - 3x \right)\]

    Step 1. Identify the structure. Chain rule, with outer \(\sec\, u\) and inner \(u = x^{2} - 3x\).

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer function.

    \[\frac{d}{du}\,\sec\, u = \sec\, u\,\tan\, u\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \sec\,\left( x^{2} - 3x \right)\tan\,\left( x^{2} - 3x \right)\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner function.

    \[u' = 2x - 3\]

    Step 4. Multiply.

    Answer

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\,\sec\,\left( x^{2} - 3x \right) = (2x - 3)\sec\,\left( x^{2} - 3x \right)\tan\,\left( x^{2} - 3x \right)\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q9c

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\frac{x^{2} - \cos(3x)}{x\, \sin(2x)}\]

    Step 1. Identify the structure. A quotient, so the quotient rule comes first:

    \[u = x^{2} - \cos(3x),\quad\quad v = x\, \sin(2x)\]

    Step 2. Differentiate \(u\), using the chain rule on the cosine.

    \[u' = 2x - \left\lbrack - \sin(3x) \cdot 3 \right\rbrack = 2x + 3\, \sin(3x)\]

    Note the double negative: the derivative of \(- \cos\) is \(+ \sin\).

    Step 3. Differentiate \(v\), which is itself a product, with a chain rule inside.

    \[v = x \cdot \sin(2x),\quad\quad v' = 1 \cdot \sin(2x) + x \cdot \left\lbrack \cos(2x) \cdot 2 \right\rbrack = \sin(2x) + 2x\, \cos(2x)\]

    Step 4. Assemble with the quotient rule.

    Answer

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \frac{u}{v} \right\rbrack = \frac{\left\lbrack 2x + 3\, \sin(3x) \right\rbrack\, x\, \sin(2x) - \left\lbrack x^{2} - \cos(3x) \right\rbrack\left\lbrack \sin(2x) + 2x\, \cos(2x) \right\rbrack}{\lbrack x\, \sin(2x)\rbrack^{2}}\]

    Step 5. Stop. The question says simplification is not required, and expanding this gains nothing. Three rules were used: quotient (outermost), product (on \(v\)), and chain (on \(\cos(3x)\), \(\sin(2x)\) and \(\cos(2x)\)).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q9d. \(\boldsymbol{\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \frac{f}{g} \right\rbrack}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x=1}\) from data

    Step 1. Write the quotient rule symbolically first.

    \[\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)'(1) = \frac{f'(1)g(1) - f(1)g'(1)}{\lbrack g(1)\rbrack^{2}}\]

    Step 2. Read off the values that are given directly.

    \[f(1) = 3,\quad\quad g(1) = 6\]

    Step 3. Evaluate \(f'(1)\) from its formula.

    \[f'(x) = \sqrt{x}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu f'(1) = \sqrt{1} = 1\]

    Step 4. Evaluate \(g'(1)\) from its formula.

    \[g'(x) = 3\,\sin\,\left( \frac{\pi x}{3} \right)\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu g'(1) = 3\,\sin\,\left( \frac{\pi}{3} \right) = 3 \cdot \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\]

    Step 5. Substitute everything.

    \[\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)'(1) = \frac{(1)(6) - (3)\left( \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2} \right)}{6^{2}} = \frac{6 - \frac{9\sqrt{3}}{2}}{36}\]

    Step 6. Clear the inner fraction. Multiply numerator and denominator by \(2\):

    \[= \frac{12 - 9\sqrt{3}}{72}\]

    Step 7. Simplify by the common factor \(3\).

    \[= \frac{3\left( 4 - 3\sqrt{3} \right)}{72} = \frac{4 - 3\sqrt{3}}{24}\]

    Answer

    \[\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)'(1) = \frac{4 - 3\sqrt{3}}{24} \approx - 0.0498\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q1a. \(\boldsymbol{y= \sin\, x\cdot \cos\,\left( \sin\,x^{2} \right)}\)

    Step 1. Identify the structure. A product of \(\sin\, x\) and \(\cos\,\left( \sin\, x^{2} \right)\).

    Step 2. Name the factors and differentiate the easy one.

    \[u = \sin\, x,\quad\quad u' = \cos\, x\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the second factor: a chain rule three layers deep.

    \[v = \cos\,\left( \sin\, x^{2} \right)\]

    Outermost is \(\cos\) of something; next is \(\sin\) of something; innermost is \(x^{2}\):

    \[v' = - \sin\,\left( \sin\, x^{2} \right) \cdot \cos\,\left( x^{2} \right) \cdot 2x\]

    Step 4. Apply the product rule.

    \[y' = \cos\, x\,\cos\,\left( \sin\, x^{2} \right) + \sin\, x \cdot \left\lbrack - 2x\,\sin\,\left( \sin\, x^{2} \right)\cos\,\left( x^{2} \right) \right\rbrack\]

    Step 5. Tidy the signs.

    Answer

    \[y' = \cos\, x\,\cos\,\left( \sin\, x^{2} \right) - 2x\,\sin\, x\,\sin\,\left( \sin\, x^{2} \right)\cos\,\left( x^{2} \right)\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q1b

    \[y = \left\lbrack \frac{1 + x^{3}}{1 - x^{2}} \right\rbrack^{1/3}\]

    Step 1. Identify the structure. A chain rule with a power outside and a quotient inside.

    \[\text{outer }u^{1/3},\quad\quad\text{inner }u = \frac{1 + x^{3}}{1 - x^{2}}\]

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer power.

    \[\frac{d}{du}u^{1/3} = \frac{1}{3}u^{- 2/3}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \frac{1}{3}\left\lbrack \frac{1 + x^{3}}{1 - x^{2}} \right\rbrack^{- 2/3}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner quotient. With \(p = 1 + x^{3}\) and \(q = 1 - x^{2}\), so \(p' = 3x^{2}\) and \(q' = - 2x\):

    \[u' = \frac{p'q - pq'}{q^{2}} = \frac{3x^{2}\left( 1 - x^{2} \right) - \left( 1 + x^{3} \right)( - 2x)}{\left( 1 - x^{2} \right)^{2}}\]

    Step 4. Simplify the inner numerator.

    \[3x^{2} - 3x^{4} + 2x + 2x^{4} = - x^{4} + 3x^{2} + 2x\]

    \[u' = \frac{- x^{4} + 3x^{2} + 2x}{\left( 1 - x^{2} \right)^{2}}\]

    Step 5. Multiply the two pieces.

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{1}{3}\left\lbrack \frac{1 + x^{3}}{1 - x^{2}} \right\rbrack^{- 2/3} \cdot \frac{- x^{4} + 3x^{2} + 2x}{\left( 1 - x^{2} \right)^{2}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q1c. \(\boldsymbol{y=\sqrt{1+\sqrt{1+x}}}\)

    Step 1. Identify the structure. Two nested square roots: chain rule twice.

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer root. With \(u = 1 + \sqrt{1 + x}\):

    \[\frac{d}{du}\sqrt{u} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{u}}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + x}}}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner expression.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left( 1 + \sqrt{1 + x} \right) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1 + x}} \cdot 1 = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1 + x}}\]

    Step 4. Multiply.

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + x}}} \cdot \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1 + x}} = \frac{1}{4\sqrt{1 + x}\,\sqrt{1 + \sqrt{1 + x}}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q1d

    \[y = \frac{\sqrt{x^{2} - 1}}{x^{2} - 2x - 8}\]

    Step 1. Identify the structure. A quotient, with a chain rule in the numerator.

    \[u = \sqrt{x^{2} - 1},\quad\quad v = x^{2} - 2x - 8\]

    Step 2. Differentiate \(u\) by the chain rule.

    \[u' = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x^{2} - 1}} \cdot 2x = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 1}}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate \(v\).

    \[v' = 2x - 2\]

    Step 4. Apply the quotient rule.

    \[y' = \frac{\frac{x}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 1}}\left( x^{2} - 2x - 8 \right) - \sqrt{x^{2} - 1}\,(2x - 2)}{\left( x^{2} - 2x - 8 \right)^{2}}\]

    Step 5. Optional tidy. Multiplying numerator and denominator by \(\sqrt{x^{2} - 1}\) clears the inner fraction:

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{x\left( x^{2} - 2x - 8 \right) - \left( x^{2} - 1 \right)(2x - 2)}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 1}\left( x^{2} - 2x - 8 \right)^{2}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q1e

    \[y = \sec^{2}\,\left\lbrack \frac{x + 1}{x - 2} \right\rbrack\]

    Step 1. Identify the structure. Three layers: a square, of a secant, of a quotient.

    Step 2. Differentiate the outermost square. Write \(y = \lbrack \sec\, w\rbrack^{2}\) with \(w = \frac{x + 1}{x - 2}\):

    \[\frac{d}{dw}\lbrack \sec\, w\rbrack^{2} = 2\,\sec\, w \cdot \sec\, w\,\tan\, w = 2\,\sec^{2}\, w\,\tan\, w\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner quotient. With \(p = x + 1\), \(q = x - 2\), so \(p' = 1\), \(q' = 1\):

    \[w' = \frac{1 \cdot (x - 2) - (x + 1) \cdot 1}{(x - 2)^{2}} = \frac{x - 2 - x - 1}{(x - 2)^{2}} = \frac{- 3}{(x - 2)^{2}}\]

    Step 4. Multiply.

    Answer

    \[y' = 2\,\sec^{2}\,\left\lbrack \frac{x + 1}{x - 2} \right\rbrack\tan\,\left\lbrack \frac{x + 1}{x - 2} \right\rbrack \cdot \frac{- 3}{(x - 2)^{2}}\]

    \[y' = \frac{- 6\,\sec^{2}\,\left\lbrack \frac{x + 1}{x - 2} \right\rbrack\tan\,\left\lbrack \frac{x + 1}{x - 2} \right\rbrack}{(x - 2)^{2}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q2a

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\frac{\tan(2x)}{\sqrt{x}}\]

    Step 1. Name the rules. Quotient rule outermost, with a chain rule on \(\tan(2x)\).

    Step 2. Differentiate the numerator.

    \[u = \tan(2x),\quad\quad u' = \sec^{2}(2x) \cdot 2 = 2\,\sec^{2}(2x)\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the denominator.

    \[v = \sqrt{x},\quad\quad v' = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}\]

    Step 4. Apply the quotient rule.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\frac{\tan(2x)}{\sqrt{x}} = \frac{2\,\sec^{2}(2x)\sqrt{x} - \tan(2x) \cdot \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}}{x}\]

    since \(v^{2} = \left( \sqrt{x} \right)^{2} = x\).

    Step 5. Optional tidy. Multiply numerator and denominator by \(2\sqrt{x}\):

    Answer

    \[= \frac{4x\,\sec^{2}(2x) - \tan(2x)}{2x\sqrt{x}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q2b. \(\boldsymbol{\frac{d}{dx}\,\cos^{3}\,\left( x^{2} \right)}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}}\)

    Step 1. Name the rule. Chain rule, twice: a cube, of a cosine, of \(x^{2}\).

    Step 2. Differentiate the cube. Write \(y = \lbrack \cos\,\left( x^{2} \right)\rbrack^{3}\):

    \[3\lbrack \cos\,\left( x^{2} \right)\rbrack^{2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}\,\cos\,\left( x^{2} \right)\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the cosine, chain rule again.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\,\cos\,\left( x^{2} \right) = - \sin\,\left( x^{2} \right) \cdot 2x = - 2x\,\sin\,\left( x^{2} \right)\]

    Step 4. Assemble the general derivative.

    \[y' = 3\,\cos^{2}\,\left( x^{2} \right) \cdot \left\lbrack - 2x\,\sin\,\left( x^{2} \right) \right\rbrack = - 6x\,\cos^{2}\,\left( x^{2} \right)\sin\,\left( x^{2} \right)\]

    Step 5. Find the inner angle at the given point.

    \[x = \frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x^{2} = \frac{\pi}{4}\]

    Step 6. Evaluate the trig values.

    \[\cos\,\left( \frac{\pi}{4} \right) = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2},\quad\quad\cos^{2}\,\left( \frac{\pi}{4} \right) = \frac{1}{2},\quad\quad\sin\,\left( \frac{\pi}{4} \right) = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\]

    Step 7. Substitute.

    \[y' = - 6 \cdot \frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2} \cdot \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} = - \frac{6\sqrt{2\pi}}{8}\]

    Step 8. Simplify.

    Answer

    \[y' = - \frac{3\sqrt{2\pi}}{4} \approx - 1.881\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. \(\boldsymbol{y=5x^{4}-3x^{3}+2x-7}\)

    Answer

    \(y' = 20x^{3} - 9x^{2} + 2\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    Problem 2

    \[y = \frac{3x - 2}{x^{2} + 1}\]

    Rule used: the quotient rule.

    Step 1. Name the pieces. \(u = 3x - 2\), \(v = x^{2} + 1\).

    Step 2. Differentiate them. \(u' = 3\), \(v' = 2x\).

    Step 3. Apply the rule.

    \[y' = \frac{3\left( x^{2} + 1 \right) - (3x - 2)(2x)}{\left( x^{2} + 1 \right)^{2}}\]

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{- 3x^{2} + 4x + 3}{\left( x^{2} + 1 \right)^{2}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    3. \(\boldsymbol{y= \sin\,\left( 3x^{2}-x \right)}\)

    Rule used: the chain rule.

    Step 1. Outer and inner. Outer \(\sin\, u\); inner \(u = 3x^{2} - x\).

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer. \(\cos\,\left( 3x^{2} - x \right)\).

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner. \(u' = 6x - 1\).

    Answer

    \(y' = (6x - 1)\cos\,\left( 3x^{2} - x \right)\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    4. \(\boldsymbol{y=\left( 2x^{3}+x-5 \right)^{5}}\)

    Rule used: the chain rule with the power rule.

    Step 1. Outer and inner. Outer \(u^{5}\); inner \(u = 2x^{3} + x - 5\).

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer. \(5\left( 2x^{3} + x - 5 \right)^{4}\).

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner. \(u' = 6x^{2} + 1\).

    Answer

    \(y' = 5\left( 2x^{3} + x - 5 \right)^{4}\left( 6x^{2} + 1 \right)\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    5. \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{2}\,\cos(3x)}\)

    Rules used: the product rule, with a chain rule on the cosine.

    Step 1. Name the factors. \(u = x^{2}\), \(v = \cos(3x)\).

    Step 2. Differentiate them.

    \[u' = 2x,\quad\quad v' = - \sin(3x) \cdot 3 = - 3\, \sin(3x)\]

    Step 3. Apply the product rule.

    \[y' = 2x\, \cos(3x) + x^{2}\left\lbrack - 3\, \sin(3x) \right\rbrack\]

    Answer

    \(y' = 2x\, \cos(3x) - 3x^{2}\, \sin(3x)\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    6. \(\boldsymbol{y=\sqrt{4x^{2}-1}}\)

    Rule used: the chain rule.

    Step 1. Outer and inner. Outer \(\sqrt{u}\); inner \(u = 4x^{2} - 1\).

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer.

    \[\frac{1}{2\sqrt{4x^{2} - 1}}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner. \(u' = 8x\).

    Step 4. Multiply and cancel the \(2\).

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{8x}{2\sqrt{4x^{2} - 1}} = \frac{4x}{\sqrt{4x^{2} - 1}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    Problem 7

    \[y = \frac{\tan\,\left( x^{2} \right)}{x}\]

    Rules used: the quotient rule, with a chain rule on the tangent.

    Step 1. Name the pieces. \(u = \tan\,\left( x^{2} \right)\), \(v = x\).

    Step 2. Differentiate them.

    \[u' = \sec^{2}\,\left( x^{2} \right) \cdot 2x = 2x\,\sec^{2}\,\left( x^{2} \right),\quad\quad v' = 1\]

    Step 3. Apply the quotient rule.

    \[y' = \frac{2x\,\sec^{2}\,\left( x^{2} \right) \cdot x - \tan\,\left( x^{2} \right) \cdot 1}{x^{2}}\]

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{2x^{2}\,\sec^{2}\,\left( x^{2} \right) - \tan\,\left( x^{2} \right)}{x^{2}}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    8. \(\boldsymbol{y= \sec(2x-1)}\)

    Rule used: the chain rule.

    Step 1. Outer and inner. Outer \(\sec\, u\); inner \(u = 2x - 1\).

    Step 2. Differentiate the outer. \(\sec(2x - 1)\tan(2x - 1)\).

    Step 3. Differentiate the inner. \(u' = 2\).

    Answer

    \(y' = 2\, \sec(2x - 1)\tan(2x - 1)\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    Problem 9

    \[\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\left\lbrack \tan(3x) \right\rbrack\]

    Step 1. First derivative, by the chain rule.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\, \tan(3x) = \sec^{2}(3x) \cdot 3 = 3\,\sec^{2}(3x)\]

    Step 2. Set up the second derivative.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack 3\,\sec^{2}(3x) \right\rbrack = 3 \cdot \frac{d}{dx}\lbrack \sec(3x)\rbrack^{2}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate the square by the chain rule.

    \[= 3 \cdot 2\, \sec(3x) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \sec(3x) \right\rbrack\]

    Step 4. Differentiate \(\sec(3x)\), chain rule again.

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\, \sec(3x) = \sec(3x)\tan(3x) \cdot 3 = 3\, \sec(3x)\tan(3x)\]

    Step 5. Substitute and collect the constants.

    \[3 \cdot 2\, \sec(3x) \cdot 3\, \sec(3x)\tan(3x) = 18\,\sec^{2}(3x)\tan(3x)\]

    Answer

    \[\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}\, \tan(3x) = 18\,\sec^{2}(3x)\tan(3x)\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents

    10. \(\boldsymbol{\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \frac{f}{g} \right\rbrack}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x=2}\) from data

    Step 1. Write the quotient rule symbolically.

    \[\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)'(2) = \frac{f'(2)g(2) - f(2)g'(2)}{\lbrack g(2)\rbrack^{2}}\]

    Step 2. Read off the given values.

    \[f(2) = 4,\quad\quad g(2) = - 1\]

    Step 3. Evaluate \(f'(2)\).

    \[f'(x) = x^{2}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu f'(2) = 4\]

    Step 4. Evaluate \(g'(2)\).

    \[g'(x) = \cos\,\left( \frac{\pi x}{2} \right)\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu g'(2) = \cos(\pi) = - 1\]

    Step 5. Substitute.

    \[\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)'(2) = \frac{(4)( - 1) - (4)( - 1)}{( - 1)^{2}} = \frac{- 4 + 4}{1}\]

    Answer

    \[\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)'(2) = 0\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 8 top☰ Contents
