    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    Every question in this type rests on one fact:

    \[\text{the derivative is the slope of the tangent line.}\]

    \[f'(a) = \text{slope of the tangent to }y = f(x)\text{ at }x = a\]

    From there the questions split into two kinds, and it is worth deciding which you have before starting.

    B1. The two question shapes

    The question asks for What to do
    the tangent line at a given \(x = a\) compute \(f(a)\) and \(f'(a)\), then use point-slope
    the \(x\) where the tangent has a given property turn the property into a target slope \(m\), then solve \(f'(x) = m\)

    The second kind is the one that catches people out, because it is an equation to solve, not a derivative to evaluate. The answer is a value of \(x\) (or several), not a line.

    B2. Turning the stated property into a target slope

    Property of the tangent Target slope \(m\)
    horizontal \(m = 0\)
    vertical \(f'(x)\) undefined (or its reciprocal is \(0\))
    parallel to a line of slope \(k\) \(m = k\)
    perpendicular to a line of slope \(k\) \(m = - \frac{1}{k}\)
    slope stated outright as \(k\) \(m = k\)
    parallel to the line through two points \(m = \frac{y_{2} - y_{1}}{x_{2} - x_{1}}\)

    The negative reciprocal is where the marks go. Perpendicular means the slopes multiply to \(- 1\):

    \[m_{1}m_{2} = - 1\mkern6mu \Leftrightarrow \mkern6mu m_{2} = - \frac{1}{m_{1}}\]

    Reading the slope \(k\) off a line in the form \(y = kx + b\) is immediate; if the line is given as \(Ax + By = C\), rearrange to \(y = \ldots\) first.

    B3. Writing the line once you have the point and the slope

    Point-slope form is the right tool, because it uses exactly what you have:

    \[y - y_{0} = m\left( x - x_{0} \right)\quad\quad\text{through }\left( x_{0},y_{0} \right)\text{ with slope }m\]

    Rearranged to \(y = mx + b\) if the question wants it in that form. The point on the curve is \(\left( a,f(a) \right)\), so \(f(a)\) has to be computed separately from \(f'(a)\). Forgetting the \(y\)-coordinate is the other common slip.

    B4. The normal line

    The normal at a point is the line through that point perpendicular to the tangent:

    \[m_{\text{normal}} = - \frac{1}{f'(a)}\quad\quad\text{(provided }f'(a) \neq 0\text{)}\]

    Same point, negative-reciprocal slope, same point-slope formula.

    B5. The procedure, written out

    1. Differentiate \(f\).
    2. Decide which shape the question is (B1).
    3. For a line at a given \(a\): compute \(f(a)\) for the point and \(f'(a)\) for the slope, then point-slope.
    4. For a required property: convert the property to a target slope \(m\) (B2), set \(f'(x) = m\), and solve. Report every solution.
    5. Sanity-check: a quadratic has exactly one point with any given tangent slope; a cubic usually has two.
    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT1 Q6. Where is the tangent to \(\boldsymbol{f(x) =3x^{2}+4x-3}\) perpendicular to \(\boldsymbol{y=6x+2}\)?

    Step 1. Recognise the question shape. It asks for the values of \(x\), not for a line. So this is the “solve \(f'(x) = m\)” kind, and the first job is to find \(m\).

    Step 2. Read the slope of the given line. The line \(y = 6x + 2\) is already in slope-intercept form, so its slope is

    \[k = 6\]

    Step 3. Convert “perpendicular” into the target slope. Perpendicular slopes are negative reciprocals:

    \[m = - \frac{1}{k} = - \frac{1}{6}\]

    Step 4. Differentiate \(f\).

    \[f(x) = 3x^{2} + 4x - 3\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu f'(x) = 6x + 4\]

    Step 5. Set the derivative equal to the target slope.

    \[6x + 4 = - \frac{1}{6}\]

    Step 6. Solve for \(x\). Subtract \(4\) from both sides:

    \[6x = - \frac{1}{6} - 4 = - \frac{1}{6} - \frac{24}{6} = - \frac{25}{6}\]

    Divide by \(6\):

    \[x = - \frac{25}{36}\]

    Answer

    \[x = - \frac{25}{36} \approx - 0.694\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. Tangent to \(\boldsymbol{f(x) =2x^{2}-5x+1}\) parallel to \(\boldsymbol{y=3x-4}\)

    Step 1. Recognise the shape. Asks for \(x\), so solve \(f'(x) = m\).

    Step 2. Read the slope of the given line. \(k = 3\).

    Step 3. Convert “parallel”. Parallel lines have equal slopes, so

    \[m = 3\]

    Step 4. Differentiate.

    \[f'(x) = 4x - 5\]

    Step 5. Solve \(f'(x) = 3\).

    \[4x - 5 = 3\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 4x = 8\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x = 2\]

    Answer

    \(x = 2\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    2. Where is the tangent to \(\boldsymbol{f(x) =x^{3}-3x}\) horizontal?

    Step 1. Convert “horizontal”. A horizontal line has slope \(0\), so \(m = 0\).

    Step 2. Differentiate.

    \[f'(x) = 3x^{2} - 3\]

    Step 3. Solve \(f'(x) = 0\).

    \[3x^{2} - 3 = 0\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 3\left( x^{2} - 1 \right) = 0\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x^{2} = 1\]

    Step 4. Take both roots.

    \[x = 1\quad\text{and}\quad x = - 1\]

    Answer

    \(x = \pm 1\). Both must be reported; giving only \(x = 1\) loses half the marks.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    3. Tangent to \(\boldsymbol{f(x) =x^{2}+2x-1}\) perpendicular to \(\boldsymbol{y= -\frac{x}{4}+2}\)

    Step 1. Read the slope of the given line.

    \[k = - \frac{1}{4}\]

    Step 2. Convert “perpendicular”.

    \[m = - \frac{1}{k} = - \frac{1}{- \frac{1}{4}} = 4\]

    Step 3. Differentiate.

    \[f'(x) = 2x + 2\]

    Step 4. Solve \(f'(x) = 4\).

    \[2x + 2 = 4\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 2x = 2\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x = 1\]

    Answer

    \(x = 1\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    4. Tangent line to \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{3}-2x}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x=1}\)

    Step 1. Recognise the shape. A specific \(x\) is given and a line is wanted, so compute the point and the slope.

    Step 2. Find the point on the curve.

    \[y(1) = 1^{3} - 2(1) = 1 - 2 = - 1\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \text{the point }(1, - 1)\]

    Step 3. Differentiate.

    \[y' = 3x^{2} - 2\]

    Step 4. Evaluate the slope at \(x = 1\).

    \[y'(1) = 3 - 2 = 1\]

    Step 5. Apply point-slope form.

    \[y - ( - 1) = 1(x - 1)\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu y + 1 = x - 1\]

    Step 6. Rearrange.

    \[y = x - 2\]

    Answer

    \(y = x - 2\).

    Check. At \(x = 1\) the line gives \(y = - 1\), matching the point on the curve. \(✓\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    5. Tangent line to \(\boldsymbol{y=\sqrt{x}}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x=4}\)

    Step 1. Find the point.

    \[y(4) = \sqrt{4} = 2\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu (4,2)\]

    Step 2. Differentiate.

    \[y' = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}\]

    Step 3. Evaluate the slope.

    \[y'(4) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{4}} = \frac{1}{4}\]

    Step 4. Apply point-slope form.

    \[y - 2 = \frac{1}{4}(x - 4)\]

    Step 5. Rearrange & answer.

    Answer

    \[y = \frac{1}{4}x - 1 + 2 = \frac{x}{4} + 1\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    6. Tangent to \(\boldsymbol{f(x) =\frac{1}{x}}\) parallel to \(\boldsymbol{y= -x+5}\)

    Step 1. Read the slope of the given line. \(k = - 1\), so parallel gives \(m = - 1\).

    Step 2. Differentiate. Rewrite as \(x^{- 1}\) and use the power rule:

    \[f'(x) = - x^{- 2} = - \frac{1}{x^{2}}\]

    Step 3. Solve \(f'(x) = - 1\).

    \[- \frac{1}{x^{2}} = - 1\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \frac{1}{x^{2}} = 1\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x^{2} = 1\]

    Step 4. Take both roots.

    \[x = 1\quad\text{and}\quad x = - 1\]

    Both are valid: neither is \(0\), so \(f\) is defined at both.

    Answer

    \(x = \pm 1\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    7. Tangent line to \(\boldsymbol{y= \sin\, x}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x=\frac{\pi}{3}}\)

    Step 1. Find the point.

    \[y\left( \frac{\pi}{3} \right) = \sin\,\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \left( \frac{\pi}{3},\ \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right)\]

    Step 2. Differentiate.

    \[y' = \cos\, x\]

    Step 3. Evaluate the slope.

    \[y'\left( \frac{\pi}{3} \right) = \cos\,\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{1}{2}\]

    Step 4. Apply point-slope form.

    \[y - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{1}{2}\left( x - \frac{\pi}{3} \right)\]

    Step 5. Rearrange into slope-intercept form.

    \[y = \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{\pi}{6} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\]

    Answer

    \[y = \frac{x}{2} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} - \frac{\pi}{6}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    8. Where is the tangent to \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{2}-4x+3}\) horizontal?

    Step 1. Note what is asked. The question wants the point(s), so both coordinates are needed, not just \(x\).

    Step 2. Convert “horizontal”. \(m = 0\).

    Step 3. Differentiate.

    \[y' = 2x - 4\]

    Step 4. Solve \(y' = 0\).

    \[2x - 4 = 0\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x = 2\]

    Step 5. Find the matching \(y\)-coordinate.

    \[y(2) = 2^{2} - 4(2) + 3 = 4 - 8 + 3 = - 1\]

    Answer

    The single point \((2, - 1)\).

    Check. This is the vertex of the parabola, which is exactly where its tangent is horizontal, and a parabola has only one. \(✓\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    9. Normal line to \(\boldsymbol{y=x^{2}}\) at \(\boldsymbol{x=1}\)

    Step 1. Find the point.

    \[y(1) = 1\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu (1,1)\]

    Step 2. Find the tangent slope.

    \[y' = 2x,\quad\quad y'(1) = 2\]

    Step 3. Convert to the normal slope. The normal is perpendicular to the tangent, so take the negative reciprocal:

    \[m_{\text{normal}} = - \frac{1}{2}\]

    Step 4. Apply point-slope form with the normal slope.

    \[y - 1 = - \frac{1}{2}(x - 1)\]

    Step 5. Rearrange.

    \[y = - \frac{1}{2}x + \frac{1}{2} + 1 = - \frac{x}{2} + \frac{3}{2}\]

    Answer

    \[y = - \frac{x}{2} + \frac{3}{2}\]

    Check. \(\left( - \frac{1}{2} \right)(2) = - 1\), so the normal really is perpendicular to the tangent. \(✓\) And at \(x = 1\) the line gives \(y = 1\), so it passes through the point. \(✓\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    10. Where does the tangent to \(\boldsymbol{f(x) =x^{3}-3x^{2}+2}\) have slope \(\boldsymbol{9}\)?

    Step 1. The target slope is given outright. \(m = 9\).

    Step 2. Differentiate.

    \[f'(x) = 3x^{2} - 6x\]

    Step 3. Set up the equation.

    \[3x^{2} - 6x = 9\]

    Step 4. Move everything to one side and divide by \(3\).

    \[3x^{2} - 6x - 9 = 0\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x^{2} - 2x - 3 = 0\]

    Step 5. Factor. Two numbers multiplying to \(- 3\) and adding to \(- 2\) are \(- 3\) and \(1\):

    \[(x - 3)(x + 1) = 0\]

    Step 6. Read both roots.

    \[x = 3\quad\text{and}\quad x = - 1\]

    Answer

    \(x = 3\) and \(x = - 1\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 9 top☰ Contents

    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    B1. The one idea

    \(y\) is a function of \(x\), even though no formula for it is available. So every time you differentiate a \(y\), the chain rule contributes a factor of \(y'\):

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\lbrack y\rbrack = y',\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack y^{2} \right\rbrack = 2y\, y',\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack y^{n} \right\rbrack = ny^{n - 1}y'\]

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \sin\, y \right\rbrack = \cos\, y \cdot y',\quad\quad\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack e^{y} \right\rbrack = e^{y}y'\]

    Differentiating an \(x\) contributes nothing extra. That asymmetry is the whole technique.

    B2. The procedure

    1. Differentiate both sides with respect to \(x\), attaching a \(y'\) every time a \(y\) is differentiated.
    2. Expand so that every term is either free of \(y'\) or linear in \(y'\).
    3. Collect all the \(y'\) terms on one side and everything else on the other.
    4. Factor out \(y'\).
    5. Divide to get \(y' = \frac{\text{everything else}}{\text{the bracket}}\).

    The answer will normally contain both \(x\) and \(y\). That is expected and correct.

    B3. The two rules that appear in almost every problem

    A term containing both \(x\) and \(y\) needs the product rule, and the \(y\) part then needs the chain rule:

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\lbrack xy\rbrack = 1 \cdot y + x \cdot y' = y + xy'\]

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack x^{3}y \right\rbrack = 3x^{2}y + x^{3}y'\]

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack xy^{2} \right\rbrack = 1 \cdot y^{2} + x \cdot 2yy' = y^{2} + 2xyy'\]

    A function of a product needs the chain rule with a product inside:

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left\lbrack \sin(xy) \right\rbrack = \cos(xy) \cdot \left( y + xy' \right)\]

    B4. Finding a tangent line from an implicit curve

    1. Differentiate implicitly and solve for \(y'\).
    2. Substitute both coordinates of the point into the expression for \(y'\). That number is the slope.
    3. Use point-slope form: \(y - y_{0} = m\left( x - x_{0} \right)\).

    A shortcut worth knowing: if the point is given, you may substitute the numbers immediately after differentiating, before solving for \(y'\). That turns the algebra into a small linear equation in \(y'\) and avoids carrying a messy general formula. MT2 Q10 below is done that way.

    B5. The mistakes that cost marks

    • Forgetting a \(y'\). Differentiating \(y^{3}\) as \(3y^{2}\) instead of \(3y^{2}y'\) is the classic error and it ruins everything downstream.
    • Forgetting the product rule on a mixed term like \(xy^{2}\).
    • Leaving \(y'\) on both sides. Step 3 exists precisely to prevent this.
    • Trying to eliminate \(y\). You usually cannot, and you are not expected to.
    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT1 Q8. \(\boldsymbol{x^{3}y+xy^{2}=4xy+7}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[3x^{2}y + x^{3}y' + y^{2} + 2xyy' = 4y + 4xy'\]

    Step 5. Move every \(y'\) term to the left and everything else to the right.

    \[x^{3}y' + 2xyy' - 4xy' = 4y - 3x^{2}y - y^{2}\]

    Step 6. Factor out \(y'\).

    \[y'\left( x^{3} + 2xy - 4x \right) = 4y - 3x^{2}y - y^{2}\]

    Step 7. Divide.

    \[y' = \frac{4y - 3x^{2}y - y^{2}}{x^{3} + 2xy - 4x}\]

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{4y - 3x^{2}y - y^{2}}{x^{3} + 2xy - 4x} = \frac{y\left( 4 - 3x^{2} - y \right)}{x\left( x^{2} + 2y - 4 \right)}\]

    The factored form on the right is optional; both are correct.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q10. Tangent to \(\boldsymbol{y^{3}+yx^{2}+x^{2}=3y^{2}}\) at \(\boldsymbol{(1,1)}\)

    Step 1. Check the point is on the curve. Always worth ten seconds:

    \[1^{3} + 1 \cdot 1^{2} + 1^{2} = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3,\quad\quad 3(1)^{2} = 3\quad ✓\]

    Step 2. Differentiate

    \[3y^{2}y' + x^{2}y' + 2xy + 2x = 6yy'\]

    Step 6. Substitute the point now, rather than solving in general. Put \(x = 1\) and \(y = 1\) into the differentiated equation:

    \[3(1)^{2}y' + (1)^{2}y' + 2(1)(1) + 2(1) = 6(1)y'\]

    \[3y' + y' + 2 + 2 = 6y'\]

    Step 7. Solve the resulting linear equation for \(y'\).

    \[4y' + 4 = 6y'\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 4 = 2y'\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu y' = 2\]

    Step 8. Write the tangent line by point-slope form. Slope \(m = 2\) through \((1,1)\):

    \[y - 1 = 2(x - 1)\]

    Step 9. Rearrange.

    \[y = 2x - 2 + 1\]

    Answer

    \[y = 2x - 1\]

    Step 10. Check. At \(x = 1\) the line gives \(y = 1\), matching the point. \(✓\)

    Substituting at Step 6 rather than solving symbolically first turned a messy rational expression into one line of arithmetic. When the point is given, take that route.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. \(\boldsymbol{x^{2}y-3xy^{2}=5}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[2xy + x^{2}y' - 3y^{2} - 6xyy' = 0\]

    Step 5. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[x^{2}y' - 6xyy' = 3y^{2} - 2xy\]

    Step 6. Factor and divide.

    Answer

    \[y'\left( x^{2} - 6xy \right) = 3y^{2} - 2xy\]

    \[y' = \frac{3y^{2} - 2xy}{x^{2} - 6xy}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    2. \(\boldsymbol{x^{3}+y^{3}=6xy}\)

    Step 1.

    \[3x^{2} + 3y^{2}y' = 6y + 6xy'\]

    Step 4. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[3y^{2}y' - 6xy' = 6y - 3x^{2}\]

    Step 5. Factor.

    \[y'\left( 3y^{2} - 6x \right) = 6y - 3x^{2}\]

    Step 6. Divide, then cancel the common factor \(3\).

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{6y - 3x^{2}}{3y^{2} - 6x} = \frac{2y - x^{2}}{y^{2} - 2x}\]

    This curve is the folium of Descartes; the answer is worth recognising.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    3. \(\boldsymbol{\sin(xy) =x+y}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[y\, \cos(xy) + x\, \cos(xy)\, y' = 1 + y'\]

    Step 4. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[x\, \cos(xy)\, y' - y' = 1 - y\, \cos(xy)\]

    Step 5. Factor.

    \[y'\left\lbrack x\, \cos(xy) - 1 \right\rbrack = 1 - y\, \cos(xy)\]

    Step 6. Divide.

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{1 - y\, \cos(xy)}{x\, \cos(xy) - 1}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    4. \(\boldsymbol{\sqrt{xy}=x+2y}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[\frac{y + xy'}{2\sqrt{xy}} = 1 + 2y'\]

    Step 4. Clear the denominator by multiplying both sides by \(2\sqrt{xy}\).

    \[y + xy' = 2\sqrt{xy}\left( 1 + 2y' \right) = 2\sqrt{xy} + 4\sqrt{xy}\, y'\]

    Step 5. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[xy' - 4\sqrt{xy}\, y' = 2\sqrt{xy} - y\]

    Step 6. Factor and divide.

    Answer

    \[y'\left( x - 4\sqrt{xy} \right) = 2\sqrt{xy} - y\]

    \[y' = \frac{2\sqrt{xy} - y}{x - 4\sqrt{xy}}\]

    Clearing the radical denominator at Step 4 is what keeps this manageable; leaving it in place makes the collection step much harder.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    5. \(\boldsymbol{x^{2}+xy+y^{2}=7}\) at the point \(\boldsymbol{(1,2)}\)

    Step 1. Check the point.

    \[1 + 2 + 4 = 7\quad ✓\]

    Step 2. Differentiate

    \[2x + y + xy' + 2yy' = 0\]

    Step 4. Collect and factor.

    \[y'(x + 2y) = - 2x - y\]

    Step 5. Solve for \(y'\) in general.

    \[y' = - \frac{2x + y}{x + 2y}\]

    Step 6. Substitute the point to get the slope.

    \[y'|_{(1,2)} = - \frac{2(1) + 2}{1 + 2(2)} = - \frac{4}{5}\]

    Step 7. Write the tangent line by point-slope form.

    \[y - 2 = - \frac{4}{5}(x - 1)\]

    Step 8. Rearrange.

    \[y = - \frac{4}{5}x + \frac{4}{5} + 2 = - \frac{4}{5}x + \frac{14}{5}\]

    Answer

    \[y' = - \frac{2x + y}{x + 2y},\quad\quad\text{tangent at }(1,2):\quad y = - \frac{4x}{5} + \frac{14}{5}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    6. \(\boldsymbol{y^{2}\,\cos\, x=x^{2}+4y}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[2y\,\cos\, x\, y' - y^{2}\,\sin\, x = 2x + 4y'\]

    Step 4. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[2y\,\cos\, x\, y' - 4y' = 2x + y^{2}\,\sin\, x\]

    Step 5. Factor and divide.

    Answer

    \[y'\left( 2y\,\cos\, x - 4 \right) = 2x + y^{2}\,\sin\, x\]

    \[y' = \frac{2x + y^{2}\,\sin\, x}{2y\,\cos\, x - 4}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    7. \(\boldsymbol{e^{xy}=x-y}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[ye^{xy} + xe^{xy}y' = 1 - y'\]

    Step 4. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[xe^{xy}y' + y' = 1 - ye^{xy}\]

    Step 5. Factor and divide.

    Answer

    \[y'\left( xe^{xy} + 1 \right) = 1 - ye^{xy}\]

    \[y' = \frac{1 - ye^{xy}}{xe^{xy} + 1}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    8. \(\boldsymbol{\tan(x+y) =xy}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[\sec^{2}(x + y) + \sec^{2}(x + y)\, y' = y + xy'\]

    Step 4. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[\sec^{2}(x + y)\, y' - xy' = y - \sec^{2}(x + y)\]

    Step 5. Factor and divide.

    Answer

    \[y'\left\lbrack \sec^{2}(x + y) - x \right\rbrack = y - \sec^{2}(x + y)\]

    \[y' = \frac{y - \sec^{2}(x + y)}{\sec^{2}(x + y) - x}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    9. \(\boldsymbol{x^{2}-4xy+y^{2}=12}\)

    Step 1. Differentiate

    \[2x - 4y - 4xy' + 2yy' = 0\]

    Step 3. Collect the \(y'\) terms.

    \[2yy' - 4xy' = 4y - 2x\]

    Step 4. Factor.

    \[y'(2y - 4x) = 4y - 2x\]

    Step 5. Divide, then cancel the common factor \(2\).

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{4y - 2x}{2y - 4x} = \frac{2y - x}{y - 2x}\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    10. \(\boldsymbol{y^{3}+x^{2}y=2}\) at the point \(\boldsymbol{(1,1)}\)

    Step 1. Check the point.

    \[1^{3} + 1^{2} \cdot 1 = 1 + 1 = 2\quad ✓\]

    Step 2. Differentiate

    \[3y^{2}y' + 2xy + x^{2}y' = 0\]

    Step 5. Collect and factor.

    \[y'\left( 3y^{2} + x^{2} \right) = - 2xy\]

    Step 6. Solve for \(y'\) in general.

    \[y' = \frac{- 2xy}{3y^{2} + x^{2}}\]

    Step 7. Substitute the point.

    \[y'|_{(1,1)} = \frac{- 2(1)(1)}{3(1) + 1} = \frac{- 2}{4} = - \frac{1}{2}\]

    Step 8. Write the tangent line.

    \[y - 1 = - \frac{1}{2}(x - 1)\]

    Step 9. Rearrange.

    \[y = - \frac{1}{2}x + \frac{1}{2} + 1 = - \frac{x}{2} + \frac{3}{2}\]

    Answer

    \[y' = \frac{- 2xy}{3y^{2} + x^{2}},\quad\quad\text{tangent at }(1,1):\quad y = - \frac{x}{2} + \frac{3}{2}\]

    Check. At \(x = 1\) the line gives \(y = - \frac{1}{2} + \frac{3}{2} = 1\) \(✓\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 10 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    B1. The procedure, which never changes

    1. Draw the picture and label every length with a variable, not a number. A quantity that changes must not be given its current value yet.
    2. List what you know and what you want, as derivatives with respect to time: \(\frac{dV}{dt}\), \(\frac{dh}{dt}\), and so on.
    3. Find an equation relating the variables. It comes from geometry: a volume formula, an area formula, the Pythagorean theorem, or similar triangles.
    4. Eliminate any variable you have no rate for, using a second relation, so the equation contains only the variables in step 2.
    5. Differentiate the whole equation with respect to \(t\), applying the chain rule to every variable.
    6. Substitute the instantaneous values now, at the very end.
    7. Solve for the unknown rate, and state it with units and a direction.

    B2. Step 1 is where the marks are lost

    Substituting a number too early is the single fatal error. If the pile is \(4\) m high at this instant, it is \(h\), not \(4\), while you differentiate. Writing \(V = \frac{\pi(4)^{3}}{12}\) makes \(V\) a constant and \(\frac{dV}{dt} = 0\), which is nonsense. The value \(4\) goes in at step 6, never before.

    B3. Step 4: the relation that eliminates the extra variable

    Most cone and shadow problems contain a variable with no given rate. Two devices remove it.

    Similar triangles, for cones and shadows. In a cone whose shape is fixed, the radius and height stay in constant proportion:

    \[\frac{r}{h} = \frac{R}{H}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu r = \frac{R}{H}\, h\]

    where \(R\) and \(H\) are the tank’s own radius and height. Substituting this into \(V = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^{2}h\) produces a formula in \(h\) alone.

    A stated relationship. “Base diameter equals height” means \(2r = h\), hence \(r = \frac{h}{2}\).

    B4. The geometry you need

    \[\text{Cone: }V = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^{2}h\quad\quad\text{Sphere: }V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^{3},\quad S = 4\pi r^{2}\]

    \[\text{Cylinder: }V = \pi r^{2}h\quad\quad\text{Circle: }A = \pi r^{2},\quad C = 2\pi r\]

    \[\text{Right triangle: }a^{2} + b^{2} = c^{2}\]

    Differentiating the Pythagorean relation gives the workhorse equation of this whole type:

    \[a^{2} + b^{2} = c^{2}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 2a\frac{da}{dt} + 2b\frac{db}{dt} = 2c\frac{dc}{dt}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu a\frac{da}{dt} + b\frac{db}{dt} = c\frac{dc}{dt}\]

    B5. Signs

    A rate is positive when its quantity is growing and negative when it is shrinking. Read the wording carefully:

    • “slides down”, “drains”, “decreases”, “hauled in” \(\Rightarrow\) negative.
    • Report the answer in words: “the top slides down at \(\frac{5}{6}\) ft/s” is better than “\(- \frac{5}{6}\) ft/s” alone, and it shows you understood the sign.

    B6. Units

    The unit of \(\frac{dQ}{dt}\) is (unit of \(Q\)) per (unit of time). Volumes give m³/min, lengths give m/min. An answer without units is incomplete.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT1 Q7. The conical gravel pile

    Step 1. Draw and name. A cone with radius \(r\) and height \(h\), both varying. Volume \(V\), also varying.

    Step 2. List the rates.

    \[\text{known: }\frac{dV}{dt} = 0.5\ \text{m}^{3}/\text{min},\quad\quad\text{want: }\frac{dh}{dt}\ \text{ when }h = 4\ \text{m}\]

    Step 3. Write the geometric relation.

    \[V = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^{2}h\]

    This has three variables but we have a rate for only two, so \(r\) must go.

    Step 4. Eliminate \(r\) using the stated relationship. “Base diameter and height are always equal” means the diameter \(2r\) equals \(h\):

    \[2r = h\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu r = \frac{h}{2}\]

    Step 5. Substitute and simplify to a formula in \(h\) alone.

    \[V = \frac{1}{3}\pi\left( \frac{h}{2} \right)^{2}h = \frac{1}{3}\pi \cdot \frac{h^{2}}{4} \cdot h = \frac{\pi h^{3}}{12}\]

    Step 6. Differentiate with respect to \(t\), chain rule on \(h^{3}\):

    \[\frac{dV}{dt} = \frac{\pi}{12} \cdot 3h^{2} \cdot \frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{\pi h^{2}}{4} \cdot \frac{dh}{dt}\]

    Step 7. Substitute the instantaneous values. Now, and only now, put \(h = 4\) and \(\frac{dV}{dt} = 0.5\):

    \[0.5 = \frac{\pi(4)^{2}}{4} \cdot \frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{16\pi}{4} \cdot \frac{dh}{dt} = 4\pi\frac{dh}{dt}\]

    Step 8. Solve.

    \[\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{0.5}{4\pi} = \frac{1}{8\pi}\]

    Answer

    \[\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{1}{8\pi} \approx 0.0398\ \text{m/min}\]

    The pile’s height is increasing at about \(4\) cm per minute. The rate is positive, as it must be for a growing pile.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q11. The rocket and the radar station

    Step 1. Draw and name. A right triangle: the horizontal leg is the fixed ground distance \(5\) mi, the vertical leg is the rocket’s height \(y\), and the hypotenuse is the radar distance \(z\). Only \(y\) and \(z\) vary.

    Step 2. List the rates.

    \[\text{known: }\frac{dz}{dt} = 2000\ \text{mi/h at the instant }y = 4,\quad\quad\text{want: }\frac{dy}{dt}\]

    Step 3. Write the relation. The \(5\) is genuinely constant, so it may be written as a number:

    \[y^{2} + 5^{2} = z^{2}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu y^{2} + 25 = z^{2}\]

    Step 4. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[2y\frac{dy}{dt} = 2z\frac{dz}{dt}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu y\frac{dy}{dt} = z\frac{dz}{dt}\]

    Step 5. Find the missing instantaneous value. The equation needs \(z\) at the moment \(y = 4\), and it is not given directly:

    \[z = \sqrt{4^{2} + 25} = \sqrt{16 + 25} = \sqrt{41}\]

    Step 6. Substitute everything.

    \[4 \cdot \frac{dy}{dt} = \sqrt{41} \cdot 2000\]

    Step 7. Solve.

    \[\frac{dy}{dt} = \frac{2000\sqrt{41}}{4} = 500\sqrt{41}\]

    Answer

    \[\frac{dy}{dt} = 500\sqrt{41} \approx 3202\ \text{mi/h}\]

    Step 8. Sanity-check. The rocket is rising faster than its distance from the radar is growing, which is right: the radar sits off to the side, so only part of the rocket’s motion is along the line of sight.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. The sliding ladder

    Step 1. Draw and name. A right triangle with the wall and the ground. Let \(x\) be the distance from the wall to the base and \(y\) the height of the top. The ladder length \(13\) is constant.

    Step 2. List the rates.

    \[\frac{dx}{dt} = 2\ \text{ft/s},\quad\quad\text{want }\frac{dy}{dt}\ \text{ when }x = 5\]

    Step 3. Write the relation.

    \[x^{2} + y^{2} = 169\]

    Step 4. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[2x\frac{dx}{dt} + 2y\frac{dy}{dt} = 0\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x\frac{dx}{dt} + y\frac{dy}{dt} = 0\]

    Step 5. Find \(y\) at the instant \(x = 5\).

    \[y = \sqrt{169 - 25} = \sqrt{144} = 12\]

    Step 6. Substitute.

    \[5(2) + 12\frac{dy}{dt} = 0\]

    Step 7. Solve.

    \[12\frac{dy}{dt} = - 10\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \frac{dy}{dt} = - \frac{10}{12} = - \frac{5}{6}\]

    Answer

    The top slides down at \(\frac{5}{6} \approx 0.833\) ft/s. The negative sign is the downward direction, exactly as expected.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    2. The inflating balloon

    Step 1. Name. Sphere of radius \(r\), volume \(V\), both varying.

    Step 2. Rates. \(\frac{dV}{dt} = 100\) cm³/s; want \(\frac{dr}{dt}\) when \(r = 5\).

    Step 3. Relation.

    \[V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^{3}\]

    Step 4. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[\frac{dV}{dt} = \frac{4}{3}\pi \cdot 3r^{2} \cdot \frac{dr}{dt} = 4\pi r^{2}\frac{dr}{dt}\]

    Step 5. Substitute.

    \[100 = 4\pi(5)^{2}\frac{dr}{dt} = 100\pi\frac{dr}{dt}\]

    Step 6. Solve.

    \[\frac{dr}{dt} = \frac{100}{100\pi} = \frac{1}{\pi}\]

    Answer

    \(\frac{dr}{dt} = \frac{1}{\pi} \approx 0.318\) cm/s.

    Note that \(4\pi r^{2}\) is the sphere’s surface area: a volume added at a steady rate spreads over the surface, so the radius grows more slowly as the balloon gets bigger.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    3. The expanding circle

    Step 1. Rates. \(\frac{dA}{dt} = 6\) cm²/s; want \(\frac{dr}{dt}\) when \(r = 3\).

    Step 2. Relation.

    \[A = \pi r^{2}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[\frac{dA}{dt} = 2\pi r\frac{dr}{dt}\]

    Step 4. Substitute.

    \[6 = 2\pi(3)\frac{dr}{dt} = 6\pi\frac{dr}{dt}\]

    Step 5. Solve.

    \[\frac{dr}{dt} = \frac{6}{6\pi} = \frac{1}{\pi}\]

    Answer

    \(\frac{1}{\pi} \approx 0.318\) cm/s.

    Here \(2\pi r\) is the circumference, the two-dimensional version of the same observation.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    4. The man and the lamppost

    Step 1. Draw and name. Let \(x\) be the man’s distance from the post and \(s\) the length of his shadow. The tip of the shadow is at distance \(x + s\) from the post.

    Step 2. Rates. \(\frac{dx}{dt} = 5\) ft/s; want \(\frac{ds}{dt}\).

    Step 3. Find the relation by similar triangles. The big triangle (post, ground to the shadow tip) and the small one (man, ground to the shadow tip) share the same angle at the tip:

    \[\frac{15}{x + s} = \frac{6}{s}\]

    Step 4. Solve that for \(s\) in terms of \(x\). Cross-multiply:

    \[15s = 6(x + s) = 6x + 6s\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 9s = 6x\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu s = \frac{2}{3}x\]

    Step 5. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[\frac{ds}{dt} = \frac{2}{3} \cdot \frac{dx}{dt}\]

    Step 6. Substitute.

    \[\frac{ds}{dt} = \frac{2}{3}(5) = \frac{10}{3}\]

    Answer

    The shadow lengthens at \(\frac{10}{3} \approx 3.33\) ft/s.

    The answer does not depend on \(x\), so it is the same wherever he is. That is a feature of the similar-triangle relation being linear.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    5. The two cars

    Step 1. Draw and name. Let \(x\) be the east car’s distance from the intersection, \(y\) the north car’s, and \(z\) the distance between them.

    Step 2. Rates. \(\frac{dx}{dt} = 80\), \(\frac{dy}{dt} = 60\) mph; want \(\frac{dz}{dt}\) after \(1\) hour.

    Step 3. Relation. The paths are perpendicular, so

    \[x^{2} + y^{2} = z^{2}\]

    Step 4. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[x\frac{dx}{dt} + y\frac{dy}{dt} = z\frac{dz}{dt}\]

    Step 5. Find the instantaneous values after one hour.

    \[x = 80(1) = 80,\quad\quad y = 60(1) = 60,\quad\quad z = \sqrt{80^{2} + 60^{2}} = \sqrt{6400 + 3600} = \sqrt{10000} = 100\]

    Step 6. Substitute.

    \[80(80) + 60(60) = 100\frac{dz}{dt}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 6400 + 3600 = 100\frac{dz}{dt}\]

    Step 7. Solve.

    \[10000 = 100\frac{dz}{dt}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu \frac{dz}{dt} = 100\]

    Answer

    The distance between them grows at \(100\) mph.

    Check. Both cars travel at constant speed in fixed perpendicular directions, so the separation grows at the constant rate \(\sqrt{80^{2} + 60^{2}} = 100\) mph at every instant, not just at \(t = 1\). \(✓\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    6. The conical water tank

    Step 1. Draw and name. A cone point-down with tank radius \(R = 2\) m and tank height \(H = 6\) m. The water forms a smaller similar cone of radius \(r\) and depth \(h\), both varying.

    Step 2. Rates. \(\frac{dV}{dt} = 3\) m³/min; want \(\frac{dh}{dt}\) when \(h = 3\).

    Step 3. Relation.

    \[V = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^{2}h\]

    Step 4. Eliminate \(r\) by similar triangles. The water cone is similar to the tank, so

    \[\frac{r}{h} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu r = \frac{h}{3}\]

    Step 5. Substitute and simplify.

    \[V = \frac{1}{3}\pi\left( \frac{h}{3} \right)^{2}h = \frac{1}{3}\pi \cdot \frac{h^{2}}{9} \cdot h = \frac{\pi h^{3}}{27}\]

    Step 6. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[\frac{dV}{dt} = \frac{\pi}{27} \cdot 3h^{2}\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{\pi h^{2}}{9}\frac{dh}{dt}\]

    Step 7. Substitute \(h = 3\).

    \[3 = \frac{\pi(3)^{2}}{9}\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{9\pi}{9}\frac{dh}{dt} = \pi\frac{dh}{dt}\]

    Step 8. Solve.

    \[\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{3}{\pi}\]

    Answer

    \(\frac{3}{\pi} \approx 0.955\) m/min.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    7. The kite

    Step 1. Draw and name. A right triangle: vertical leg is the constant height \(100\) ft, horizontal leg is \(x\), hypotenuse is the string length \(z\).

    Step 2. Rates. \(\frac{dx}{dt} = 8\) ft/s; want \(\frac{dz}{dt}\) when \(z = 200\).

    Step 3. Relation.

    \[x^{2} + 100^{2} = z^{2}\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu x^{2} + 10000 = z^{2}\]

    Step 4. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[x\frac{dx}{dt} = z\frac{dz}{dt}\]

    Step 5. Find \(x\) when \(z = 200\).

    \[x = \sqrt{200^{2} - 100^{2}} = \sqrt{40000 - 10000} = \sqrt{30000} = 100\sqrt{3}\]

    Step 6. Substitute.

    \[100\sqrt{3} \cdot 8 = 200\frac{dz}{dt}\]

    Step 7. Solve.

    \[\frac{dz}{dt} = \frac{800\sqrt{3}}{200} = 4\sqrt{3}\]

    Answer

    String pays out at \(4\sqrt{3} \approx 6.93\) ft/s.

    Less than the kite’s \(8\) ft/s horizontal speed, which is right: part of the motion is across the line of the string rather than along it.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    8. The boat and the pulley

    Step 1. Draw and name. A right triangle: vertical leg is the constant \(4\) m from pulley to water, horizontal leg is the boat’s distance \(x\) from the dock, hypotenuse is the rope length \(z\).

    Step 2. Rates. The rope is being hauled in, so it is getting shorter:

    \[\frac{dz}{dt} = - 2\ \text{m/min},\quad\quad\text{want }\frac{dx}{dt}\ \text{ when }z = 5\]

    Step 3. Relation.

    \[x^{2} + 16 = z^{2}\]

    Step 4. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[x\frac{dx}{dt} = z\frac{dz}{dt}\]

    Step 5. Find \(x\) when \(z = 5\).

    \[x = \sqrt{25 - 16} = \sqrt{9} = 3\]

    Step 6. Substitute.

    \[3\frac{dx}{dt} = 5( - 2) = - 10\]

    Step 7. Solve.

    \[\frac{dx}{dt} = - \frac{10}{3}\]

    Answer

    The boat approaches the dock at \(\frac{10}{3} \approx 3.33\) m/min. The negative sign means \(x\) is shrinking, that is, the boat is getting closer, which is what “approaches” means.

    Note the boat moves faster than the rope is hauled, because the rope is at an angle.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    9. The streetlight and the shadow tip

    Step 1. Draw and name. Let \(x\) be the person’s distance from the base of the light and \(s\) the shadow’s length. The tip of the shadow is at distance \(x + s\) from the base, and it is the tip’s speed that is wanted.

    Step 2. Rates. \(\frac{dx}{dt} = 1.2\) m/s; want \(\frac{d}{dt}(x + s)\).

    Step 3. Similar triangles.

    \[\frac{5}{x + s} = \frac{1.8}{s}\]

    Step 4. Solve for \(s\).

    \[5s = 1.8(x + s) = 1.8x + 1.8s\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu 3.2s = 1.8x\mkern6mu \Rightarrow \mkern6mu s = \frac{1.8}{3.2}x = \frac{9}{16}x\]

    Step 5. Write the tip’s position.

    \[x + s = x + \frac{9}{16}x = \frac{25}{16}x\]

    Step 6. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[\frac{d}{dt}(x + s) = \frac{25}{16} \cdot \frac{dx}{dt}\]

    Step 7. Substitute.

    \[\frac{25}{16}(1.2) = \frac{25}{16} \cdot \frac{6}{5} = \frac{150}{80} = \frac{15}{8}\]

    Answer

    The shadow’s tip moves at \(\frac{15}{8} = 1.875\) m/s.

    The distinction to be careful about. The shadow’s length grows at \(\frac{9}{16}(1.2) = 0.675\) m/s; the shadow’s tip moves at \(1.875\) m/s. The question asked for the tip, so the person’s own motion has to be added in. Answering \(0.675\) is the standard trap here.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 11 top☰ Contents

    10. The melting snowball

    Step 1. Rates. The radius is decreasing, so its rate is negative:

    \[\frac{dr}{dt} = - 0.2\ \text{cm/min},\quad\quad\text{want }\frac{dV}{dt}\ \text{ when }r = 10\]

    Step 2. Relation.

    \[V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^{3}\]

    Step 3. Differentiate with respect to \(t\).

    \[\frac{dV}{dt} = 4\pi r^{2}\frac{dr}{dt}\]

    Step 4. Substitute.

    \[\frac{dV}{dt} = 4\pi(10)^{2}( - 0.2) = 4\pi(100)( - 0.2)\]

    Step 5. Evaluate.

    \[= - 80\pi\]

    Answer

    The volume is decreasing at \(80\pi \approx 251.3\) cm³/min.

    The sign is carried correctly from \(\frac{dr}{dt}\) through the calculation, and the word “decreasing” in the answer is what shows the sign was understood rather than dropped.

    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    The key idea is to replace a hard value \(f(x)\) by the tangent-line approximation near a convenient base point \(a\) where \(f(a)\) is easy to evaluate.

    Linearization formula

    \[L(x) = f(a) + f'(a)(x - a).\]

    Differentials

    \[dy = f'(x)\, dx,\]

    and near \(x = a\),

    \[\Delta y \approx dy = f'(a)\, dx,\]

    where

    \[dx = x - a.\]

    Procedure

    1. Choose a nearby base point \(a\) with an easy exact value.
    2. Compute \(f(a)\) and \(f'(a)\).
    3. Compute the signed change \(dx = x - a\).
    4. Use \(L(x) = f(a) + f'(a)dx\) or \(f(x) \approx f(a) + dy\).
    5. Keep full precision during the work and round only at the end.

    Things to remember

    • For angle changes measured in degrees, convert the change to radians:
    \[1^{\circ} = \frac{\pi}{180}.\]
    • For a square root,
    \[\frac{d}{dx}\sqrt{x} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}.\]
    • For a power,
    \[\frac{d}{dx}x^{n} = nx^{n - 1}.\]
    • For a reciprocal,
    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left( \frac{1}{x} \right) = - \frac{1}{x^{2}}.\]
    • A linearization is an approximation, not an equality.

    Common traps

    Choosing a base point that is not easy, forgetting the sign of \(dx\), treating degrees as radians, and rounding intermediate values too early are the common sources of lost marks.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    MT1 Q9 - Approximate \(\boldsymbol{\sqrt{9.2}}\)

    Step 1. Let

    \[f(x) = \sqrt{x}.\]

    Choose \(a = 9\) because

    \[f(9) = \sqrt{9} = 3.\]

    Step 2. Differentiate:

    \[f'(x) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}},\quad\quad f'(9) = \frac{1}{6}.\]

    Step 3. Compute the change in \(x\):

    \[dx = 9.2 - 9 = 0.2.\]

    Step 4. Compute the differential:

    \[dy \approx f'(9)\, dx = \frac{1}{6}(0.2) = \frac{1}{30}.\]

    Step 5. Add the change to the base value:

    \[\sqrt{9.2} \approx 3 + \frac{1}{30} = 3.033333\ldots\]

    Answer

    \[\sqrt{9.2} \approx 3.0333\]

    The calculator value is about \(3.03315\), so the linear estimate is close.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    MT2 Q8 - Estimate \(\boldsymbol{\sin\left( 62^{\circ} \right)}\)

    Step 1. Use

    \[f(x) = sinx\]

    with the convenient base point

    \[a = 60^{\circ} = \frac{\pi}{3}.\]

    Step 2. Convert the change to radians:

    \[dx = 62^{\circ} - 60^{\circ} = 2^{\circ} = \frac{\pi}{90}.\]

    Step 3. Evaluate the base value and derivative:

    \[f(a) = \sin\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},\]

    \[f'(a) = \cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{1}{2}.\]

    Step 4. Form the linearization:

    \[L(x) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \frac{1}{2}\left( x - \frac{\pi}{3} \right).\]

    Step 5. Since \(62^{\circ} = 31\pi/90\),

    \[L\left( \frac{31\pi}{90} \right) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \frac{\pi}{90}.\]

    Step 6. Numerically,

    \[\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \frac{\pi}{90} \approx 0.88348.\]

    Answer

    \[\sin\left( 62^{\circ} \right) \approx 0.8835\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q2(a) - Estimate \(\boldsymbol{\cos\left( 62^{\circ} \right)}\)

    Use \(f(x) = cosx\) at \(a = \pi/3\).

    \[dx = \frac{2\pi}{180} = \frac{\pi}{90}.\]

    Because

    \[f'(x) = - sinx,\]

    we have

    \[f'\left( \frac{\pi}{3} \right) = - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}.\]

    Therefore

    \[dy \approx - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\frac{\pi}{90} = - \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{180}.\]

    Thus

    \[\cos\left( 62^{\circ} \right) \approx \frac{1}{2} - \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{180} \approx 0.469770.\]

    Answer

    \[\cos\left( 62^{\circ} \right) \approx 0.4698\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q2(b) - Estimate \(\boldsymbol{\sqrt{16.4}}\)

    Choose \(a = 16\) because \(\sqrt{16} = 4\).

    \[f'(x) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}},\quad\quad f'(16) = \frac{1}{8}.\]

    The change is

    \[dx = 16.4 - 16 = 0.4.\]

    So

    \[dy \approx \frac{1}{8}(0.4) = 0.05.\]

    Hence

    \[\sqrt{16.4} \approx 4 + 0.05 = 4.0500.\]

    Answer

    \[\sqrt{16.4} \approx 4.0500\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. Approximate \(\boldsymbol{\sqrt{50}}\)

    Choose \(f(x) = \sqrt{x}\) and \(a = 49\).

    \[f(49) = 7,\quad\quad f'(49) = \frac{1}{14}.\]

    Since

    \[dx = 50 - 49 = 1,\]

    we get

    \[dy \approx \frac{1}{14}.\]

    Therefore

    \[\sqrt{50} \approx 7 + \frac{1}{14} = 7.071428\ldots\]

    Answer

    \(7.0714\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    2. Approximate \(\boldsymbol{\sqrt[3]{26}}\)

    Let \(f(x) = x^{1/3}\) and \(a = 27\).

    \[f(27) = 3,\quad\quad f'(x) = \frac{1}{3}x^{- 2/3},\quad\quad f'(27) = \frac{1}{27}.\]

    The change is

    \[dx = 26 - 27 = - 1.\]

    Hence

    \[dy \approx - \frac{1}{27},\]

    so

    \[\sqrt[3]{26} \approx 3 - \frac{1}{27} = 2.962963\ldots\]

    Answer

    \(2.9630\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    3. Approximate \(\boldsymbol{\left( 1.97 \right)^{4}}\)

    Take \(f(x) = x^{4}\) and \(a = 2\).

    \[f(2) = 16,\quad\quad f'(2) = 4(2)^{3} = 32.\]

    Since

    \[dx = 1.97 - 2 = - 0.03,\]

    we have

    \[dy \approx 32( - 0.03) = - 0.96.\]

    Thus

    Answer

    \[(1.97)^{4} \approx 16 - 0.96 = 15.0400.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    4. Approximate \(\boldsymbol{\cos\left( 29^{\circ} \right)}\)

    Use \(f(x) = cosx\) and \(a = 30^{\circ} = \pi/6\).

    \[dx = - 1^{\circ} = - \frac{\pi}{180}.\]

    Also

    \[f(a) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},\quad\quad f'(a) = - \frac{1}{2}.\]

    Therefore

    \[dy \approx \left( - \frac{1}{2} \right)\left( - \frac{\pi}{180} \right) = \frac{\pi}{360}.\]

    So

    \[\cos\left( 29^{\circ} \right) \approx \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \frac{\pi}{360} \approx 0.874752.\]

    Answer

    \(0.8748\)

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 12 top☰ Contents

    5. Approximate \(\boldsymbol{\tan\left( 46^{\circ} \right)}\)

    The change is

    \[dx = 1^{\circ} = \frac{\pi}{180}.\]

    Hence

    \[\tan\left( 46^{\circ} \right) \approx 1 + 2\frac{\pi}{180} = 1 + \frac{\pi}{90} \approx 1.034907.\]

    \[dy \approx \frac{1}{10}( - 0.5) = - 0.05.\]

    Therefore

    10. Approximate \(\boldsymbol{\left( 8.1 \right)^{2/3}}\)

    \[dy \approx \frac{1}{3}(0.1) = \frac{1}{30}.\]

    Thus

    Problem 10

    Solution ↓
    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 13 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    Newton’s method replaces the equation \(f(x) = 0\) by repeated tangent-line corrections.

    Newton iteration

    \[x_{n + 1} = x_{n} - \frac{f\left( x_{n} \right)}{f'\left( x_{n} \right)}.\]

    Procedure

    1. Define \(f(x)\) and calculate \(f'(x)\).
    2. Use the supplied starting value \(x_{0}\).
    3. Substitute into the Newton formula to obtain \(x_{1}\).
    4. Repeat with \(x_{1}\) to obtain \(x_{2}\), and so on.
    5. Keep extra digits during intermediate calculations.
    6. Check that \(f\left( x_{n} \right)\) is getting closer to \(0\) and the iterates are stabilizing.

    Things to remember

    1. Newton’s method requires \(f'\left( x_{n} \right) \neq 0\).
    2. A good initial guess is usually close to the desired root.
    3. A stable-looking sequence is evidence of convergence, but the check \(f\left( x_{n} \right) \rightarrow 0\) is also important.
    4. When the problem specifies an interval, use the root in that interval.
    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 13 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solution

    F1 Q7 - \(\boldsymbol{x^{4}+2x-5=0}\)

    Step 1. Let

    \[f(x) = x^{4} + 2x - 5,\]

    so

    \[f'(x) = 4x^{3} + 2.\]

    Step 2. Start with \(x_{0} = 1.5\).

    Step 3. Apply Newton’s formula:

    \[x_{1} = 1.5 - \frac{(1.5)^{4} + 2(1.5) - 5}{4(1.5)^{3} + 2} \approx 1.302419.\]

    Step 4. Repeat:

    \[x_{2} = x_{1} - \frac{x_{1}^{4} + 2x_{1} - 5}{4x_{1}^{3} + 2} \approx 1.257919.\]

    Step 5. One more iteration gives

    \[x_{3} \approx 1.255941.\]

    Answer

    \[x \approx 1.25594\]

    Substitution gives a value of \(f(x)\) very close to \(0\), confirming the stabilized approximation.

    14Curve Sketching with Calculus

    QuestionsF1 Q412345678910
    MethodProcedureThings to remember
    Exam solutions(a) \(\boldsymbol{f\(b) \(\boldsymbol{f\
    Practice solutions12345678910

    Part A - The Questions

    F1 Q4 · 20 pts

    Sketch the graph of each function below. State all critical points, cusps, vertical asymptotes, and points of inflection.

    1. \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{x^{2}}{x^{2} - 1}}\)

    2. \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = x + sinx}\)

    Solution ↓

    10 New Practice Problems

    Problem 1

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{x^{2}}{x^{2} - 4}}\): find critical points, asymptotes, and inflection points.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 2

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = x + cosx}\) on the real line: find critical points and inflection points.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 3

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = x^{1/3}(x - 4)}\): find the critical point at \(\boldsymbol{x = 0}\) and classify it.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 4

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = (x - 1)^{2/3}}\): classify the point at \(\boldsymbol{x = 1}\).

    Solution ↓
    Problem 5

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{x}{x^{2} + 1}}\): find critical points, asymptotes, and inflection points.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 6

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = x^{4} - 4x^{3}}\): find critical points and inflection points.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 7

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \sqrt{x^{2} - 4}}\): find the domain, critical points, and end behavior.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 8

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{x^{2}}{x - 3}}\): find critical points and the slant asymptote.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 9

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = 2x^{3} - 3x^{2} - 12x + 5}\): find critical points and inflection points.

    Solution ↓
    Problem 10

    Analyze \(\boldsymbol{f(x) = \frac{1}{x^{2} - 1}}\): find critical points and asymptotes.

    Solution ↓
    ↑ Top of Part A↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    Curve sketching is a checklist problem. Work in a fixed order.

    Procedure

    • Find the domain and identify vertical asymptotes or excluded points.
    • Compute \(f'(x)\) and locate critical numbers where \(f'(x) = 0\) or \(f'(x)\) does not exist while \(f\) is defined.
    • Use a sign chart for \(f'\) to determine increasing/decreasing behavior and classify local extrema.
    • Compute \(f''(x)\) and locate candidates for inflection points.
    • Use a sign chart for \(f''\) to determine concavity.
    • Find horizontal or slant asymptotes from end behavior.
    • Mark important points and asymptotes, then sketch each branch.

    Things to remember

    1. A point where \(f'\) is undefined is a critical number only when the function itself is defined there.
    2. A vertical asymptote is not a critical point because the function is not defined there.
    3. A cusp has opposite one-sided infinite slopes.
    4. A vertical tangent has the same one-sided infinite slope sign.
    5. An inflection point requires a change in concavity, not merely \(f''(c) = 0\).
    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    (a) \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\frac{x^{2}}{x^{2}-1}}\)

    Step 1. Domain and vertical asymptotes.

    \[x^{2} - 1 = 0\quad \Rightarrow \quad x = \pm 1.\]

    So the domain excludes \(x = \pm 1\), and the vertical asymptotes are

    Answer

    \[x = - 1,\quad x = 1.\]

    Step 2. First derivative.

    Using the quotient rule,

    \[f'(x) = \frac{2x\left( x^{2} - 1 \right) - x^{2}(2x)}{\left( x^{2} - 1 \right)^{2}} = - \frac{2x}{\left( x^{2} - 1 \right)^{2}}.\]

    Thus the only critical number is \(x = 0\).

    \[f(0) = 0.\]

    Because the denominator of \(f'\) is positive wherever the function is defined, \(f'(x) > 0\) for \(x < 0\) and \(f'(x) < 0\) for \(x > 0\). Hence \((0,0)\) is a local maximum.

    Step 3. Horizontal asymptote.

    The numerator and denominator have the same degree, so

    \[\lim_{x \rightarrow \pm \infty}f(x) = 1.\]

    Therefore

    Answer

    \[y = 1\]

    is the horizontal asymptote.

    Step 4. Concavity.

    \[f''(x) = \frac{2\left( 3x^{2} + 1 \right)}{\left( x^{2} - 1 \right)^{3}}.\]

    The numerator is always positive. Concavity is therefore up for \(|x| > 1\) and down for \(|x| < 1\).

    There is no inflection point because \(x = \pm 1\) are not in the domain and the sign change occurs across asymptotes.

    Sketch information: vertical asymptotes \(x = \pm 1\), horizontal asymptote \(y = 1\), and a local maximum at \((0,0)\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    (b) \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x+ sinx}\)

    \[f'(x) = 1 + cosx \geq 0.\]

    Therefore \(f\) is increasing everywhere. The derivative is zero at

    \[x = (2k + 1)\pi,\quad\quad k\mathbb{\in Z,}\]

    but the sign of \(f'\) does not change, so these points are not local extrema.

    Next,

    \[f''(x) = - sinx.\]

    Inflection candidates satisfy

    \[\sin x = 0\quad \Rightarrow \quad x = k\pi.\]

    Since \(- sinx\) changes sign at every \(k\pi\), every

    Answer

    \[x = k\pi\]

    is an inflection point. The corresponding points are

    Answer

    \[(k\pi,k\pi).\]

    There are no vertical asymptotes, and the graph stays within \(1\) unit of the line \(y = x\) because

    \[- 1 \leq sinx \leq 1.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\frac{x^{2}}{x^{2}-4}}\)

    \[f'(x) = - \frac{8x}{\left( x^{2} - 4 \right)^{2}}.\]

    Thus \(x = 0\) is the only critical number and

    \[f(0) = 0.\]

    Since the denominator is positive, \(f'\) changes from positive to negative at \(0\), so \((0,0)\) is a local maximum.

    The denominator is zero at \(x = \pm 2\), giving vertical asymptotes

    \[x = \pm 2.\]

    Equal degrees give the horizontal asymptote

    \[y = 1.\]

    Also,

    \[f''(x) = \frac{24x^{2} + 8}{\left( x^{2} - 4 \right)^{3}} > 0\]

    on each part of its domain, so there are no inflection points.

    Answer

    critical point \((0,0)\); VAs \(x = \pm 2\); HA \(y = 1\); no inflection points.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    2. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x+ cosx}\)

    \[f'(x) = 1 - sinx \geq 0.\]

    Critical numbers occur when

    \[\sin x = 1\quad \Rightarrow \quad x = \frac{\pi}{2} + 2k\pi.\]

    The derivative does not change sign, so these are horizontal-tangent plateau points, not extrema.

    Next,

    \[f''(x) = - cosx.\]

    The concavity changes when

    \[\cos x = 0,\]

    so the inflection points occur at

    \[x = \frac{\pi}{2} + k\pi.\]

    Answer

    critical points at \(x = \pi/2 + 2k\pi\); inflection points at \(x = \pi/2 + k\pi\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    3. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x^{1/3}\left( x-4 \right)}\)

    Rewrite:

    \[f(x) = x^{4/3} - 4x^{1/3}.\]

    Then

    \[f'(x) = \frac{4}{3}x^{1/3} - \frac{4}{3}x^{- 2/3} = \frac{4(x - 1)}{3x^{2/3}}.\]

    At \(x = 0\), \(f'\) is undefined but \(f(0) = 0\), so \(x = 0\) is a critical number. The one-sided derivative tends to \(- \infty\) on both sides, so the tangent is vertical with the same sign from both sides.

    Therefore the point is a vertical tangent, not a cusp.

    Also,

    \[f'(1) = 0,\]

    and \(f'\) changes from negative to positive at \(x = 1\), giving a local minimum there:

    \[f(1) = - 3.\]

    Answer

    vertical tangent at \((0,0)\); local minimum at \((1, - 3)\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    4. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\left( x-1 \right)^{2/3}}\)

    \[f'(x) = \frac{2}{3}(x - 1)^{- 1/3}.\]

    As \(x \rightarrow 1^{-}\),

    \[f'(x) \rightarrow - \infty,\]

    while as \(x \rightarrow 1^{+}\),

    Answer

    \[f'(x) \rightarrow + \infty.\]

    The one-sided slopes have opposite signs, so \((1,0)\) is a genuine cusp. Because \(f(x) \geq 0\), it is also the absolute minimum.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    5. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}}\)

    \[f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^{2}}{\left( x^{2} + 1 \right)^{2}}.\]

    Critical numbers are

    \[x = \pm 1.\]

    The sign of \(f'\) gives a local minimum at \(( - 1, - 1/2)\) and a local maximum at \((1,1/2)\).

    There is no vertical asymptote because \(x^{2} + 1 > 0\) for all real \(x\). Since the denominator has higher degree,

    \[y = 0\]

    is the horizontal asymptote.

    For concavity,

    \[f''(x) = \frac{2x\left( x^{2} - 3 \right)}{\left( x^{2} + 1 \right)^{3}}.\]

    Thus the inflection candidates are

    Answer

    \[x = - \sqrt{3},\quad x = 0,\quad x = \sqrt{3},\]

    and the sign of \(f''\) changes at each one. Hence all three are inflection points.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    6. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x^{4}-4x^{3}}\)

    \[f'(x) = 4x^{2}(x - 3).\]

    Critical numbers are \(x = 0\) and \(x = 3\).

    At \(x = 0\), \(f'\) is negative on both sides, so there is a horizontal-tangent plateau rather than an extremum.

    At \(x = 3\), \(f'\) changes from negative to positive, so \(\left( 3,f(3) \right) = (3, - 27)\) is a local minimum.

    Next,

    \[f''(x) = 12x(x - 2).\]

    Concavity changes at \(x = 0\) and \(x = 2\), so both are inflection points:

    Answer

    \[(0,0),\quad(2, - 16).\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    7. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\sqrt{x^{2}-4}}\)

    For real outputs,

    \[x^{2} - 4 \geq 0,\]

    so the domain is

    Answer

    \[\left( - \infty, - 2\rbrack \cup \lbrack 2,\infty \right).\]

    For \(|x| > 2\),

    \[f'(x) = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 4}}.\]

    The derivative is undefined at the endpoints, which are boundary critical candidates because the function is defined there.

    As \(x \rightarrow \infty\),

    \[\sqrt{x^{2} - 4} \sim x,\]

    so the right-hand slant asymptote is \(y = x\). Similarly, on the left,

    \[f(x) \sim - x,\]

    giving the left-hand slant asymptote \(y = - x\).

    There is no vertical asymptote because \(f( \pm 2) = 0\) is finite.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    8. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\frac{x^{2}}{x-3}}\)

    Long division gives

    \[\frac{x^{2}}{x - 3} = x + 3 + \frac{9}{x - 3}.\]

    Therefore the vertical asymptote is

    Answer

    \[x = 3,\]

    and the slant asymptote is

    Answer

    \[y = x + 3.\]

    Using the quotient rule,

    \[f'(x) = \frac{x(x - 6)}{(x - 3)^{2}}.\]

    Thus the critical numbers are

    \[x = 0,\quad x = 6.\]

    The function values are \(f(0) = 0\) and \(f(6) = 12\); the sign of \(f'\) shows a local maximum at \((0,0)\) and a local minimum at \((6,12)\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    9. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=2x^{3}-3x^{2}-12x+5}\)

    \[f'(x) = 6x^{2} - 6x - 12 = 6(x - 2)(x + 1).\]

    Critical numbers are

    \[x = - 1,\quad x = 2.\]

    The sign pattern gives a local maximum at \(x = - 1\) and a local minimum at \(x = 2\).

    Values:

    \[f( - 1) = 12,\quad\quad f(2) = - 13.\]

    Next,

    \[f''(x) = 12x - 6.\]

    Set \(f''(x) = 0\):

    \[x = \frac{1}{2}.\]

    Since \(f''\) changes sign there, the inflection point is

    Answer

    \[\left( \frac{1}{2},\frac{5}{2} \right).\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 14 top☰ Contents

    10. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\frac{1}{x^{2}-1}}\)

    The domain excludes \(x = \pm 1\), so

    Answer

    \[x = - 1,\quad x = 1\]

    are vertical asymptotes.

    \[f'(x) = - \frac{2x}{\left( x^{2} - 1 \right)^{2}}.\]

    Thus \(x = 0\) is the only critical number, with

    \[f(0) = - 1.\]

    The derivative changes from positive to negative, so \((0, - 1)\) is a local maximum.

    Since the denominator has higher degree,

    Answer

    \[y = 0\]

    is the horizontal asymptote. There are no inflection points in the domain.

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    Extreme Value Theorem checklist

    If \(f\) is continuous on a closed interval \(\lbrack a,b\rbrack\), then it attains both an absolute maximum and an absolute minimum.

    To find them:

    • Compute \(f'(x)\).
    • Find every critical number in \((a,b)\) where \(f'(x) = 0\) or \(f'\) is undefined while \(f\) is defined.
    • Evaluate \(f\) at every critical number and both endpoints.
    • Compare all values.

    Optimization checklist

    • Draw or visualize the geometry and name the variables.
    • Write the constraint equation.
    • Solve the constraint for one variable.
    • Write the quantity to optimize as a single-variable function.
    • Find the critical point.
    • Check that it gives the required minimum or maximum, usually by sign change, the endpoint behavior, or the geometry.
    • State the dimensions and units clearly.

    Formulas to remember

    Rectangle:

    \[A = xy,\quad\quad P = 2x + 2y.\]

    Cylinder:

    \[V = \pi r^{2}h,\]

    \[S = 2\pi r^{2} + 2\pi rh.\]

    Average value:

    \[f_{\text{avg}} = \frac{1}{b - a}\int_{a}^{b}f(x)\, dx.\]

    Common traps

    Do not omit critical numbers where \(f'\) is undefined. Do not optimize two independent variables when the constraint can reduce the problem to one variable. Always compare endpoints in an absolute-extrema problem.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    F1 Q5(a) - \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=2x^{5/3}-5x^{4/3}}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -1,20 \right\rbrack}\)

    Differentiate:

    \[f'(x) = \frac{10}{3}x^{2/3} - \frac{20}{3}x^{1/3} = \frac{10}{3}x^{1/3}\left( x^{1/3} - 2 \right).\]

    Critical numbers:

    \[x = 0,\quad\quad x = 8.\]

    Evaluate at endpoints and critical numbers:

    \[f( - 1) = 2( - 1)^{5/3} - 5( - 1)^{4/3} = - 7,\]

    \[f(0) = 0,\]

    \[f(8) = 2(8)^{5/3} - 5(8)^{4/3} = - 48,\]

    and

    \[f(20) \approx 23.2808.\]

    The largest value is \(23.2808\) at \(x = 20\); the smallest is \(- 48\) at \(x = 8\).

    Answer

    absolute maximum \(f(20) \approx 23.2808\); absolute minimum \(f(8) = - 48\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q5(b) - \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x+ cosx}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -\pi,2\pi \right\rbrack}\)

    \[f'(x) = 1 - sinx.\]

    Critical numbers occur where

    \[\sin x = 1\quad \Rightarrow \quad x = \frac{\pi}{2}.\]

    Evaluate:

    \[f( - \pi) = - \pi - 1,\]

    \[f\left( \frac{\pi}{2} \right) = \frac{\pi}{2},\]

    \[f(2\pi) = 2\pi + 1.\]

    The derivative is never negative, so the function is increasing on the interval.

    Answer

    absolute minimum \(- \pi - 1\) at \(x = - \pi\); absolute maximum \(2\pi + 1\) at \(x = 2\pi\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q6 - Rectangle of area \(\boldsymbol{220\text{ cm}^{2}}\)

    Let the sides be \(x\) and \(y\).

    Constraint:

    \[xy = 220\quad \Rightarrow \quad y = \frac{220}{x}.\]

    Perimeter:

    \[P = 2x + 2y = 2x + \frac{440}{x}.\]

    Differentiate:

    \[P'(x) = 2 - \frac{440}{x^{2}}.\]

    Set \(P'(x) = 0\):

    \[2 = \frac{440}{x^{2}}\quad \Rightarrow \quad x^{2} = 220,\]

    so

    \[x = y = \sqrt{220} = 2\sqrt{55}.\]

    The minimum perimeter is

    \[P = 4\sqrt{220} = 8\sqrt{55} \approx 59.33\text{ cm}.\]

    Answer

    \(2\sqrt{55}\text{ cm} \times 2\sqrt{55}\text{ cm}\), perimeter \(8\sqrt{55}\text{ cm}\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q3(a) - \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=1/\left( x^{2}+1 \right)}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -1,1 \right\rbrack}\)

    \[f'(x) = - \frac{2x}{\left( x^{2} + 1 \right)^{2}}.\]

    The only critical number is \(x = 0\).

    Evaluate:

    \[f( - 1) = \frac{1}{2},\quad\quad f(0) = 1,\quad\quad f(1) = \frac{1}{2}.\]

    Therefore

    Answer

    \[\text{absolute maximum} = 1\text{ at }x = 0\]

    and

    Answer

    \[\text{absolute minimum} = \frac{1}{2}\text{ at }x = \pm 1.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q3(b) - Bound the integral

    On \(\lbrack - 1,1\rbrack\),

    \[\frac{1}{2} \leq \frac{1}{x^{2} + 1} \leq 1.\]

    The interval length is \(2\), so

    \[2\left( \frac{1}{2} \right) \leq \int_{- 1}^{1}\frac{1}{x^{2} + 1}\, dx \leq 2(1).\]

    Thus

    Answer

    \[1 \leq \int_{- 1}^{1}\frac{1}{x^{2} + 1}\, dx \leq 2.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q5 - Cheapest rectangular box

    Let the width be \(w\). Then the length is \(2w\).

    Volume constraint:

    \[2wh = 120\quad \Rightarrow \quad h = \frac{60}{w^{2}}.\]

    The four side faces have total area

    \[2\ellh + 2wh = 2(2w)h + 2wh = 6wh.\]

    At \(1.20\) dollars/cm\(^{2}\), the side cost is

    \[7.20wh.\]

    The base area is \(2w^{2}\), so the base cost is

    \[2.40w^{2}.\]

    The lid also has area \(2w^{2}\), so the lid cost is

    \[3.00w^{2}.\]

    Therefore

    \[C(w) = 5.40w^{2} + 7.20w\left( \frac{60}{w^{2}} \right) = 5.40w^{2} + \frac{432}{w}.\]

    Differentiate:

    \[C'(w) = 10.80w - \frac{432}{w^{2}}.\]

    Set equal to zero:

    \[10.80w^{3} = 432\quad \Rightarrow \quad w^{3} = 40.\]

    Thus

    \[w = \sqrt[3]{40} \approx 3.420.\]

    Then

    \[\mathcal{l =}2\sqrt[3]{40} \approx 6.840\text{ cm},\]

    \[h = \frac{60}{\left( \sqrt[3]{40} \right)^{2}} \approx 5.127\text{ cm}.\]

    Since

    \[C''(w) = 10.80 + \frac{864}{w^{3}} > 0,\]

    this critical point gives the minimum.

    Using \(w^{3} = 40\),

    \[C_{\min} = 5.40w^{2} + \frac{432}{w} = 5.40w^{2} + 10.80w^{2} = 16.20w^{2} \approx 189.74.\]

    Answer

    \[w = \sqrt[3]{40} \approx 3.420\text{ cm},\quad\mathcal{l \approx}6.840\text{ cm},\quad h \approx 5.127\text{ cm}\]

    with minimum cost

    Answer

    \[\$ 189.74\text{ (approximately)}.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q7(a) - \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\left( x^{2}+2x \right)^{2/3}}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -2,3 \right\rbrack}\)

    Differentiate:

    \[f'(x) = \frac{2}{3}\left( x^{2} + 2x \right)^{- 1/3}(2x + 2).\]

    Critical numbers occur at

    \[x = - 2,\quad x = - 1,\quad x = 0,\]

    where the function is defined, together with the endpoint \(x = 3\) for the absolute-extrema test.

    Evaluate:

    \[f( - 2) = 0,\quad\quad f( - 1) = 1,\quad\quad f(0) = 0,\]

    and

    \[f(3) = 15^{2/3}.\]

    Since \(15^{2/3} \approx 6.082\), the absolute maximum occurs at \(x = 3\), while the absolute minimum is \(0\) at \(x = - 2\) and \(x = 0\).

    Answer

    maximum \(15^{2/3}\) at \(x = 3\); minimum \(0\) at \(x = - 2,0\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q7(b) - Bound the integral

    On \(\lbrack - 2,3\rbrack\),

    \[0 \leq \left( x^{2} + 2x \right)^{2/3} \leq 15^{2/3}.\]

    The interval length is \(5\), hence

    Answer

    \[0 \leq \int_{- 2}^{3}\left( x^{2} + 2x \right)^{2/3}\, dx \leq 5 \cdot 15^{2/3}.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    Part D - Worked Solutions: New Practice Problems

    1. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x^{4}-8x^{2}+3}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -3,3 \right\rbrack}\)

    \[f'(x) = 4x^{3} - 16x = 4x(x - 2)(x + 2).\]

    Critical numbers are \(x = - 2,0,2\).

    Evaluate:

    \[f( - 3) = 12,\quad f( - 2) = - 13,\quad f(0) = 3,\quad f(2) = - 13,\quad f(3) = 12.\]

    Answer

    absolute maximum \(12\) at \(x = \pm 3\); absolute minimum \(- 13\) at \(x = \pm 2\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    2. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x+2\cosx}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack 0,2\pi \right\rbrack}\)

    \[f'(x) = 1 - 2\sin x.\]

    Thus

    \[\sin x = \frac{1}{2},\]

    giving

    \[x = \frac{\pi}{6},\quad\frac{5\pi}{6}.\]

    Compare

    \[f(0) = 2,\]

    \[f\left( \frac{\pi}{6} \right) = \frac{\pi}{6} + \sqrt{3},\]

    \[f\left( \frac{5\pi}{6} \right) = \frac{5\pi}{6} - \sqrt{3},\]

    \[f(2\pi) = 2\pi + 2.\]

    Answer

    absolute maximum \(2\pi + 2\) at \(2\pi\); absolute minimum \(5\pi/6 - \sqrt{3}\) at \(5\pi/6\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    3. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=1/\left( x^{2}+4 \right)}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -2,2 \right\rbrack}\)

    \[f'(x) = - \frac{2x}{\left( x^{2} + 4 \right)^{2}}.\]

    Critical number: \(x = 0\).

    Compare

    \[f(0) = \frac{1}{4},\quad\quad f( \pm 2) = \frac{1}{8}.\]

    Answer

    absolute maximum \(1/4\) at \(x = 0\); absolute minimum \(1/8\) at \(x = \pm 2\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    4. Rectangle of area \(\boldsymbol{300\text{ cm}^{2}}\)

    With \(xy = 300\),

    \[y = \frac{300}{x}.\]

    Then

    \[P(x) = 2x + \frac{600}{x},\]

    so

    \[P'(x) = 2 - \frac{600}{x^{2}} = 0\quad \Rightarrow \quad x^{2} = 300.\]

    Thus the rectangle is a square:

    Answer

    \[x = y = 10\sqrt{3}\text{ cm}.\]

    Minimum perimeter:

    Answer

    \[40\sqrt{3}\text{ cm} \approx 69.28\text{ cm}.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    5. Box with \(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{l =}3w}\) and volume \(\boldsymbol{150\text{ cm}^{3}}\)

    The constraint gives

    \[3wh = 150\quad \Rightarrow \quad h = \frac{50}{w^{2}}.\]

    Base area is \(3w^{2}\); lid area is \(3w^{2}\); four sides have area

    \[2(3wh) + 2(wh) = 8wh.\]

    With the stated costs,

    \[C(w) = 8wh + 3w^{2} + 2\left( 3w^{2} \right) = 9w^{2} + 400/w.\]

    Differentiate:

    \[C'(w) = 18w - \frac{400}{w^{2}}.\]

    Set \(C'(w) = 0\):

    \[18w^{3} = 400\quad \Rightarrow \quad w^{3} = \frac{200}{9}.\]

    Hence

    \[w \approx 2.811,\quad\quad\mathcal{l =}3w \approx 8.434,\quad\quad h = \frac{50}{w^{2}} \approx 6.326.\]

    The minimum cost is about \(213.41\) dollars.

    Answer

    \(w \approx 2.811\text{ cm},\mkern6mu \mathcal{l \approx}8.434\text{ cm},\mkern6mu h \approx 6.326\text{ cm}\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    6. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=\left( x^{2}-4x \right)^{2/3}}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -1,5 \right\rbrack}\)

    The expression is defined on the whole interval using the real branch.

    Critical numbers occur at the zeros of \(x^{2} - 4x = x(x - 4)\) and at the derivative-zero point obtained from the outer factor, giving

    \[x = 0,\quad 2,\quad 4.\]

    Compare

    \[f( - 1) = 5^{2/3},\quad f(0) = 0,\quad f(2) = 4^{2/3},\quad f(4) = 0,\quad f(5) = 5^{2/3}.\]

    Answer

    absolute maximum \(5^{2/3}\) at \(x = - 1,5\); absolute minimum \(0\) at \(x = 0,4\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    7. Closest point on \(\boldsymbol{y=\sqrt{x}}\) to \(\boldsymbol{\left( 4,0 \right)}\)

    A point on the curve has coordinates \(\left( x,\sqrt{x} \right)\).

    Minimize squared distance:

    \[D^{2} = (x - 4)^{2} + x.\]

    Differentiate:

    \[\frac{d}{dx}D^{2} = 2(x - 4) + 1 = 2x - 7.\]

    Set equal to zero:

    \[2x - 7 = 0\quad \Rightarrow \quad x = \frac{7}{2}.\]

    Then

    \[y = \sqrt{\frac{7}{2}} = \frac{\sqrt{14}}{2}.\]

    Answer

    \[\left( \frac{7}{2},\frac{\sqrt{14}}{2} \right).\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    8. Closed cylinder with volume \(\boldsymbol{54\pi}\)

    From

    \[\pi r^{2}h = 54\pi,\]

    we get

    \[h = \frac{54}{r^{2}}.\]

    Surface area:

    \[S = 2\pi r^{2} + 2\pi rh = 2\pi r^{2} + \frac{108\pi}{r}.\]

    Differentiate:

    \[S'(r) = 4\pi r - \frac{108\pi}{r^{2}}.\]

    Set equal to zero:

    \[4r^{3} = 108\quad \Rightarrow \quad r^{3} = 27,\]

    so

    \[r = 3,\quad\quad h = 6.\]

    Answer

    \(r = 3\text{ cm},\mkern6mu h = 6\text{ cm}\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    9. \(\boldsymbol{f\left( x \right)=x^{5/3}-5x^{2/3}}\) on \(\boldsymbol{\left\lbrack -1,8 \right\rbrack}\)

    \[f'(x) = \frac{5}{3}x^{- 1/3}(x - 2).\]

    Critical numbers are \(x = 0\) and \(x = 2\).

    Evaluate:

    \[f( - 1) = - 6,\quad f(0) = 0,\quad f(2) = - 3 \cdot 2^{2/3},\quad f(8) = 12.\]

    Since \(- 3 \cdot 2^{2/3} \approx - 4.762\),

    Answer

    absolute maximum \(12\) at \(x = 8\); absolute minimum \(- 6\) at \(x = - 1\). The point \(x = 0\) is a cusp/local maximum, while \(x = 2\) is a local minimum.

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 15 top☰ Contents

    10. Field of area \(\boldsymbol{480\text{ m}^{2}}\) divided into two pens

    Let \(a\) be the side parallel to the divider and \(b\) the other side. The area constraint is

    \[ab = 480\quad \Rightarrow \quad b = \frac{480}{a}.\]

    The total fencing uses three lengths of \(a\) and two lengths of \(b\):

    \[L(a) = 3a + 2b = 3a + \frac{960}{a}.\]

    Differentiate:

    \[L'(a) = 3 - \frac{960}{a^{2}}.\]

    Set \(L'(a) = 0\):

    \[3 = \frac{960}{a^{2}}\quad \Rightarrow \quad a^{2} = 320,\]

    so

    \[a = 8\sqrt{5} \approx 17.89.\]

    Then

    \[b = \frac{480}{8\sqrt{5}} = 12\sqrt{5} \approx 26.83.\]

    Minimum total fence:

    \[L_{\min} = 48\sqrt{5} \approx 107.33\text{ m}.\]

    Answer

    \[8\sqrt{5}\text{ m} \times 12\sqrt{5}\text{ m}\]

    with minimum fencing

    Answer

    \[48\sqrt{5}\text{ m}.\]

    16Antiderivatives and Integration Techniques

    MethodBasic antiderivative rulesUseful trig identitiesCommon traps
    Exam solutionsF1 Q8aF1 Q8bF1 Q8cF1 Q8dF1 Q8eF2 Q6aF2 Q6bF2 Q6cF2 Q6d

    Part A - The Questions

    F1 Q8 · 20 pts

    Compute the value of each integral.

    \[\boldsymbol{\int\left( x^{2} - x \right)\sqrt{3x}\, dx}\]

    \[\boldsymbol{\int \sin(2x)\cos x\, dx}\]

    \[\boldsymbol{\int_{a}^{b}(x + \cos2x)\, dx}\]

    \[\boldsymbol{\int\left( x^{2} - 4 \right)^{2}\, dx}\]

    \[\boldsymbol{\int_{2}^{4}x\sqrt{x - 1}\, dx}\]

    Part B - How to Solve This Type

    Basic antiderivative rules

    \[\int x^{n}\, dx = \frac{x^{n + 1}}{n + 1} + C,\quad\quad n \neq - 1.\]

    \[\int cosx\, dx = sinx + C,\]

    \[\int sinx\, dx = - cosx + C.\]

    For a substitution \(u = g(x)\),

    \[du = g'(x)\, dx,\]

    and the goal is to rewrite the integral entirely in terms of \(u\).

    Useful trig identities

    \[\sin A\cos B = \frac{1}{2}\left\lbrack \sin(A + B) + \sin(A - B) \right\rbrack,\]

    \[\cos A\sin B = \frac{1}{2}\left\lbrack \sin(A + B) - \sin(A - B) \right\rbrack.\]

    Also remember

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left( \sec x \right) = secx\tan x,\]

    \[\frac{d}{dx}\left( \tan x \right) = \sec^{2}x.\]

    Common traps

    Expand algebraic products before applying the power rule when needed; do not forget \(+ C\) on indefinite integrals; and for definite integrals, evaluate the upper endpoint minus the lower endpoint after finding an antiderivative.

    ↑ Top of Part B↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    Part C - Worked Solutions

    F1 Q8(a)

    \[\int\left( x^{2} - x \right)\sqrt{3x}\, dx = \sqrt{3}\int\left( x^{2} - x \right)x^{1/2}\, dx.\]

    Distribute:

    \[= \sqrt{3}\int\left( x^{5/2} - x^{3/2} \right)\, dx.\]

    Apply the power rule:

    \[= \sqrt{3}\left( \frac{2}{7}x^{7/2} - \frac{2}{5}x^{5/2} \right) + C.\]

    Answer

    \[\frac{2\sqrt{3}}{7}x^{7/2} - \frac{2\sqrt{3}}{5}x^{5/2} + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q8(b)

    Use product-to-sum:

    \[\sin(2x)\cos x = \frac{1}{2}\left\lbrack \sin(3x) + sinx \right\rbrack.\]

    Therefore

    \[\int \sin(2x)\cos x\, dx = - \frac{1}{6}\cos(3x) - \frac{1}{2}\cos x + C.\]

    Answer

    \[- \frac{1}{6}\cos(3x) - \frac{1}{2}\cos x + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q8(c)

    \[\int_{a}^{b}(x + \cos2x)\, dx = \left\lbrack \frac{x^{2}}{2} + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2x \right\rbrack_{a}^{b}.\]

    Thus

    Answer

    \[\frac{b^{2} - a^{2}}{2} + \frac{\sin 2b - \sin2a}{2}.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q8(d)

    Expand:

    \[\left( x^{2} - 4 \right)^{2} = x^{4} - 8x^{2} + 16.\]

    Then

    \[\int\left( x^{4} - 8x^{2} + 16 \right)\, dx = \frac{x^{5}}{5} - \frac{8}{3}x^{3} + 16x + C.\]

    Answer

    \(x^{5}/5 - (8/3)x^{3} + 16x + C\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F1 Q8(e)

    Let

    \[u = x - 1,\quad\quad x = u + 1,\quad\quad du = dx.\]

    The bounds change from \(x = 2,4\) to \(u = 1,3\).

    Then

    \[\int_{2}^{4}x\sqrt{x - 1}\, dx = \int_{1}^{3}(u + 1)u^{1/2}\, du.\]

    So

    \[= \int_{1}^{3}\left( u^{3/2} + u^{1/2} \right)\, du\]

    \[= \left\lbrack \frac{2}{5}u^{5/2} + \frac{2}{3}u^{3/2} \right\rbrack_{1}^{3} = \frac{84\sqrt{3} - 16}{15}.\]

    Answer

    \(\left( 84\sqrt{3} - 16 \right)/15\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q6(a)

    Use

    \[u = \sqrt{2x}.\]

    Then

    \[x = \frac{u^{2}}{2},\quad\quad dx = u\, du,\]

    and

    \[\sqrt{x} = \frac{u}{\sqrt{2}}.\]

    Hence

    \[\int\frac{\cos\left( \sqrt{2x} \right)}{\sqrt{x}}\, dx = \sqrt{2}\int cosu\, du = \sqrt{2}\sin u + C.\]

    Therefore

    Answer

    \[\sqrt{2}\sin\left( \sqrt{2x} \right) + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q6(b)

    Let

    \[u = tanx,\quad\quad du = \sec^{2}x\, dx.\]

    The bounds become \(u = 0\) and \(u = \sqrt{3}\).

    Thus

    \[\int_{0}^{\pi/3}\tan x\sec^{2}x\, dx = \int_{0}^{\sqrt{3}}u\, du = \frac{1}{2}\left( \sqrt{3} \right)^{2} = \frac{3}{2}.\]

    Answer

    \(3/2\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q6(c)

    Divide every term by \(x^{2}\):

    \[\frac{x^{3} + \sqrt{5x} - 4}{x^{2}} = x + \sqrt{5}\, x^{- 3/2} - 4x^{- 2}.\]

    Integrate term by term:

    \[\frac{x^{2}}{2} - 2\frac{\sqrt{5}}{\sqrt{x}} + \frac{4}{x} + C.\]

    Answer

    \[\frac{x^{2}}{2} - \frac{2\sqrt{5}}{\sqrt{x}} + \frac{4}{x} + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part C↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    F2 Q6(d)

    Let

    \[u = secx,\quad\quad du = secx\tan x\, dx.\]

    Then

    \[\sec^{3}x\tan x\, dx = \sec^{2}x\left( \sec x\tan x\, dx \right) = u^{2}\, du.\]

    Therefore

    \[\int u^{2}\, du = \frac{u^{3}}{3} + C,\]

    so

    Answer

    \[\frac{1}{3}\sec^{3}x + C.\]

    \[\cos(3x)\sin x = \frac{1}{2}\left\lbrack \sin(4x) - \sin(2x) \right\rbrack.\]

    Thus

    Answer

    \[- \frac{1}{8}\cos(4x) + \frac{1}{4}\cos(2x) + C.\]

    3. \(\boldsymbol{\int_{a}^{b}\left( x+ \sin3x \right)\, dx}\)

    \[\int(x + \sin3x)\, dx = \frac{x^{2}}{2} - \frac{1}{3}\cos 3x.\]

    Therefore

    Answer

    \[\frac{b^{2} - a^{2}}{2} + \frac{\cos 3a - \cos3b}{3}.\]

    4. \(\boldsymbol{\int\left( x^{2}-9 \right)^{2}\, dx}\)

    Expand:

    \[\left( x^{2} - 9 \right)^{2} = x^{4} - 18x^{2} + 81.\]

    Integrate:

    Answer

    \[\frac{x^{5}}{5} - 6x^{3} + 81x + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    5. \(\boldsymbol{\int_{1}^{5}x\sqrt{x-1}\, dx}\)

    Let \(u = x - 1\). Then \(x = u + 1\) and the bounds are \(1\) to \(4\).

    \[\int_{1}^{5}x\sqrt{x - 1}\, dx = \int_{0}^{4}(u + 1)u^{1/2}\, du.\]

    So

    \[= \left\lbrack \frac{2}{5}u^{5/2} + \frac{2}{3}u^{3/2} \right\rbrack_{0}^{4} = \frac{272}{15}.\]

    Answer

    \(272/15\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    6. \(\boldsymbol{\int\frac{\cos\left( \sqrt{3x} \right)}{\sqrt{x}}\, dx}\)

    Let \(u = \sqrt{3x}\). Then \(x = u^{2}/3\) and \(dx = 2u/3\, du\).

    Since \(\sqrt{x} = u/\sqrt{3}\),

    \[\int\frac{\cos\left( \sqrt{3x} \right)}{\sqrt{x}}\, dx = \frac{2\sqrt{3}}{3}\int cosu\, du.\]

    Thus

    Answer

    \[\frac{2\sqrt{3}}{3}\sin\left( \sqrt{3x} \right) + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    7. \(\boldsymbol{\int_{0}^{\pi/4}\sec^{2}x\tanx\, dx}\)

    Let \(u = tanx\), \(du = \sec^{2}x\, dx\).

    Bounds: \(u = 0\) to \(u = 1\).

    \[\int_{0}^{1}u\, du = \frac{1}{2}.\]

    Answer

    \(1/2\).

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    8. \(\boldsymbol{\int\frac{x^{4}+\sqrt{7x}-3}{x^{2}}\, dx}\)

    Rewrite:

    \[x^{2} + \sqrt{7}\, x^{- 3/2} - 3x^{- 2}.\]

    Integrate:

    Answer

    \[\frac{x^{3}}{3} - \frac{2\sqrt{7}}{\sqrt{x}} + \frac{3}{x} + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    9. \(\boldsymbol{\int\sec^{4}x\tanx\, dx}\)

    Let \(u = secx\), so \(du = secx\tan x\, dx\).

    Then

    \[\sec^{4}x\tan x\, dx = u^{3}\, du,\]

    so

    Answer

    \[\frac{1}{4}\sec^{4}x + C.\]

    ‹ PreviousQuestionNext ›↑ Top of Part D↑ Ch 16 top☰ Contents

    10. \(\boldsymbol{\int_{0}^{\pi/6}\tan\left( 2x \right)\sec^{2}\left( 2x \right)\, dx}\)

    Let

    \[u = \tan(2x),\quad\quad du = 2\sec^{2}(2x)\, dx.\]

    Thus

    \[\int_{0}^{\pi/6}\tan(2x)\sec^{2}(2x)\, dx = \frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\sqrt{3}}u\, du = \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{3}{2} = \frac{3}{4}.\]

    Answer

    \(3/4\).
